A relative reference where `allow_relative` is withdrawn, a scheme outside `schemes`, and a fragment where `allow_fragment` is withdrawn are validation errors — the token is inside the family's lexical space and outside the type's value space — and a character outside the family's grammar is refused by the family's parser (§7.4). The value of each is its text as written.
- **`hostname` is a domain name, and the value is the name** ([TSON-DATA] §5.5): RFC 5890's labels, LDH labels, A-labels or U-labels in any mix. ASCII case is lexical, and a U-label and its A-label are two spellings of one label, each recovered from the other by Punycode (RFC 3492), so `Example.COM` and `example.com` are one value and so are `xn--bcher-kva.example` and `bücher.example`. The canonical text is the lowercase U-label form — what a writer emits, and the form an identity's host takes ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1). `net.hostname_type` carries one facet beside its `spec`: `allow_idn`, a **permission** (§5.7) granted by default, which withdrawn admits only a name none of whose labels is a U-label or an A-label. It judges the value and not a spelling, so `!hostname ^ { allow_idn: false }` refuses `bücher.example` and `xn--bcher-kva.example` alike, as a validation error — the token is inside the family's lexical space and outside the type's value space — while a token outside RFC 5890's grammar (uppercase beyond ASCII, a trailing dot, a last label beginning with a digit) is refused by the family's parser (§7.4).
- **`host` is a host name or an IP address, never both** ([TSON-DATA] §5.5): RFC 3987's `ihost` profiled to DNS, whose lexical space is the union of `hostname`'s, `ipv4`'s and `ipv6`'s plus an IPv6 address in brackets. Each member keeps its own equality — a name compares as a `hostname` value, an address numerically — and a name never equals an address, nor an IPv4 address an IPv6 one, its IPv4-mapped form included. The brackets are a spelling and not a value, so `[::1]` and `::1` are one host. The canonical text is the member's: lowercase U-labels, a dotted quad, or RFC 5952 text unbracketed. `net.host_type` has no facets beyond its `spec`: a position that wants only names, or only addresses, is typed by the member, `hostname`, `ipv4` or `ipv6`.
- **`media_type` is a type, a subtype and a map of parameters** ([TSON-DATA] §5.5): an RFC 6838 media type, its parameters RFC 9110 §8.3.1's. The type, subtype and parameter names compare without ASCII case, parameter order carries no meaning, a quoted parameter value equals its token form, and a parameter value keeps its case except `charset`'s, which folds. The canonical text is the lowercase type, subtype and parameter names with the `charset` value folded, parameters sorted by name, each value a token where it is one and quoted otherwise, joined by `;` with no whitespace: `text/html;charset=utf-8`. `net.media_type_type` carries three facets beside its `spec`. `allow_parameters` is a **permission** (§5.7), withheld by default; withheld, a value carrying a parameter is a validation error. Net's `media_type` admits none, and since a refinement may withdraw a permission and never grant one, a position that takes a `Content-Type` value declares its own instance, `content_type => !net.media_type_type { allow_parameters: true }`. `types` lists the admitted top-level types, and `suffixes` RFC 6839's structured-syntax suffixes, a suffix `s` admitting a subtype ending `+s` and the subtype `s` itself; each list, where it is given, must admit the value, and a value outside one is a validation error. Both are **member sets** (§5.7) whose entries are media-type names and compare as the type and subtype do, without ASCII case — so `suffixes: [JSON]` admits `application/ld+json` and `application/json`, and `types: [Text]` refines `types: [text image]`: the family's own value identity that §5.7's subset rule compares by is the media type's, not that of the lists' element type `text`. Each entry MUST be an RFC 6838 `restricted-name`, a suffix holding no `+`, and a list MUST NOT be empty, an empty one admitting nothing; a body breaking either is a schema-load coherence error.
- **`time` and `datetime` are instants.** RFC 3339 makes the offset mandatory, and the offset is a *spelling*: a `datetime` is the instant on the UTC timeline and a `time` is the time of day in UTC on `[00:00:00, 24:00:00)`, so `2026-01-01T10:00:00+01:00` and `2026-01-01T09:00:00Z` are one value, `-00:00` (offset unknown, RFC 3339 §4.3) is the same instant as `Z`, and `23:30:00-02:00` is `01:30:00Z`. A leap second — second 60, which RFC 3339's grammar admits — is neither a time of day on that interval nor an instant on that timeline, and both families' parsers refuse it ([TSON-DATA] §5.4). Both families are totally ordered — the mandatory offset is what makes them so; XSD's partial order is the cost of an optional one. A wall clock with no offset, and an appointment for which the zone is data, are other value spaces and not these types. TSON text preserves the offset as written; an encoding that stores the instant may drop it. A bound on `temporal.time_type` is a `time`, on `temporal.datetime_type` a `datetime`.
- **`duration` is a signed exact decimal number of seconds, and `period` a signed integer number of months.** The split is what gives each a total order: a month has no fixed length beside a second that has one, so the two are two value spaces rather than one partially ordered one, and every ordered-bound facet on them is enforceable (§5.7). `duration`'s lexical form is RFC 3339 Appendix A's `duration` production with an optional leading `-`, a fraction on the seconds component only, any subset of `D`/`H`/`M`/`S` in order with at least one present, and no `Y` or month-`M`; `PnW` stands alone, a week is 7 days and a day 86400 s, so `PT90M`, `PT1H30M` and `P0DT5400S` are one value and `PT0S`, `P0D` and `-PT0S` are one value. `period`'s is `P` with a `Y` component, an `M` component, or both, and nothing else. Both ends of `duration`'s space are fixed at a signed 64-bit count of nanoseconds: **`time-secfrac` is `"." 1*9DIGIT`** — stated once, for `duration`, `time` and `datetime` alike, since no host runtime represents finer than a nanosecond and a value below that floor is not exactness anyone can use — and **a `duration`'s magnitude does not exceed 2⁶³ − 1 ns** (about 292 years), as a magnitude so that negating an admitted value yields an admitted one; a processor MUST represent every value in that range and MUST reject one outside it whether or not its own representation could hold it. A span of centuries is a calendar span and is a `period`; a quantity finer or wider than the range is a `number` in the unit the schema names. The floor is lexical (a fraction reaches the value through one component and is capped at the token) and the ceiling is a value rule (the seconds count is summed from `D`, `H`, `M` and `S`, so `P400000D` overflows with no single component long enough to catch). A bound or step on `temporal.duration_type` is a `duration`, on `temporal.period_type` a `period`.
- **`precision`** on `temporal.time_type`, `temporal.datetime_type` and `temporal.duration_type` is a constraint on the *value*, not on a spelling: `precision: N` admits a value that is a whole number of 10⁻ᴺ seconds — `fraction_digits` on the seconds count — so `precision: 3` is millisecond resolution, `precision: 0` whole seconds, and `precision: 9` the nanosecond grid; reading admits any spelling of an admitted value, trailing zeros included, so `12:00:00.500` is admitted under `precision: 1`, and a text encoding writing the value writes at most N fractional digits, `12:00:00.5`. The atom is exact — nothing is truncated; a value off the grid is rejected. `precision` may not exceed 9, which falls out of the fraction production rather than being stated twice; a larger value is a schema-load coherence error. The temporal families carry no timezone facet: RFC 3339 `full-time` and `date-time`, which the atoms' `spec` pins, already make the offset mandatory, so a facet requiring it would be vacuous and one relaxing it would widen the atom against its own pin. All five temporal families state their bounds as §5.11 groups, `( min | exclusive_min )?` and `( max | exclusive_max )?`, so a half-open interval is spellable on a date exactly as on an integer.
- **The network families' `within`/`excluding` pair MUST admit a value**, and the rule is exact. `net.ipv4_type`, `net.ipv6_type`, `net.cidr4_type` and `net.cidr6_type` each carry `within` (a value must lie inside some listed network; a network must be a subnet of one) and `excluding` (an address must lie inside none; a network must not *overlap* any — overlap, not containment, so a wider value cannot smuggle an excluded block), each list typed by the family's network instance, `[cidr4]` or `[cidr6]`, so a malformed entry or one with nonzero host bits is refused at schema load by that reader, as any value of it would be. A body whose two lists between them admit nothing is invalid at the schema level, in the voice the family already uses for a prefix outside its range: `{ within: ["10.0.0.0/8"]  excluding: ["10.0.0.0/8"] }` is `{ min: 10  max: 3 }` in another spelling, and one an author reaches by editing. The check is decidable exactly — CIDR blocks are nested or disjoint and never partly overlap, so an exclusion meeting a permitted block either contains it or lies wholly inside one of its halves, and the walk terminates at the address width — and a resolver MUST implement it exactly rather than pairwise, since `10.0.0.0/9` and `10.128.0.0/9` cover `10.0.0.0/8` by counting and only the tiling case arises from a real edit. **For a network family the prefix bounds participate**: a value must be a subnet of a `within` block, so its prefix is at least that block's, and `max_prefix` caps it from above; `{ within: ["10.0.0.0/24"]  excluding: ["10.0.0.5/32"]  max_prefix: 24 }` admits almost every *address* and no *network* at all, since the one block the prefix bounds leave overlaps the hole. The diagnostic SHOULD say which cause it found — an exclusion covering everything permitted, or the largest block the bounds leave — since the two want different edits.


### 5.6 Canonical Form and Desugaring

All type-definition bodies ultimately take a single canonical form:

```
!C { bindings }
```

where `C` names a constructor and `bindings` is a record literal filling the constructor's fields. Every other form — inline type expressions, positional constructor forms, value refinements — is syntactic sugar that desugars to this form during resolution; resolver output always records the fully expanded canonical form as the entry's value.

**Positional form.** When a constructor has exactly one field whose name is unmarked — a field the document must write, whatever its modifier (§5.2) — the core value after `!C` may be that field's value directly:

```
!enum [true false]       →  !enum { members: [true false] }
!meta.array text         →  !meta.array { element_type: text }
!meta.choice [a b]       →  !meta.choice { variants: [a b] }
```

Fields whose name carries `?` do not count, whether or not they carry a value. The positional form is invalid when the type has zero, two, or more unmarked names — the resolver MUST reject such uses with a clear error. This restriction applies to the positional form only.

**The positional form is general over schema-backed data.** At any data position whose declared type is a record with exactly one unmarked-name field, a non-brace-delimited value fills that field directly; a braced value is always the explicit record (so record- and map-valued fills cannot go positional — the same exclusion as above). The two spellings denote the same value; which one canonical output uses is a per-type convention (§8.1). Type-definition bodies canonicalise the other way — toward the explicit bindings record, per the end state below — a deliberate pair of conventions: bodies uniform, references minimal. **In a schema document the positional form keeps a bare token where the positional field is atom-typed** — a type's name at a `meta.type_ref` slot, an enum member, a literal — **and loses it where that field is product- or sum-typed**: there the explicit record is the only spelling, since a bare token at a record- or choice-typed position in a schema document would otherwise be indistinguishable from a name, and a value written by name stands at a slot typed by a reference naming values and nowhere else (§4.2, §5.12); a bare token at a record-typed slot whose positional field is product- or sum-typed, or at one whose record has no positional form, is refused as no record. So `fields: { p => text }` fills `meta.record_field.type`, an atom-typed positional field, in a schema document as in a data document (§8.1). A data document has no namespace to resolve a name in and reads every bare token positionally, whatever the positional field's type, so a data document's positional fills read as they always have. **An application is data grammar**: at a reference-typed slot in a data document — a `parents` list in resolver output, say — `result<T>` is the reference value [TSON-DATA] §7.4 reads, written unquoted, and at a reference-typed slot of a *value body* — a field of a namespace entry's map (§4.1) — an application is refused outright: nothing lifts or mints in a value body, so the application would have no entry to be the name of. The spelling that gives such an application a name is the alias, `order_page => page<order>` (§8.2).

**Record-bindings form.** `!C { ... }` is the explicit form, valid for any constructor as long as the bindings cover every field whose name is unmarked. Empty bindings `!C {}` are valid whenever the constructor has no unmarked-name field.

**Value refinement.** `!I ^ { values }` desugars by retargeting to the instance's constructor, **with `values` merged over `I`'s own already-bound field values**: a field named in `values` overrides `I`'s value for it, and every field `I` itself bound that `values` does not mention keeps `I`'s value — the atom counterpart of §5.7's body-materialisation rule, under which inherited constraints survive a refinement that does not mention them:

```
!integer ^ { min: 0  max: 150 }   →  !numeric.integer_type { min: 0  max: 150 }
!text ^ { min_length: 1 }         →  !unicode.text_type { min_length: 1 }
```

For a fresh instance (`integer => !numeric.integer_type {}`) the merge is a no-op — every inherited field is absent — so the single-hop desugar above is the general rule's degenerate case. Chained refinement is where the merge matters:

```
int8      => !integer ^ { size: { bits: 8  signed: true } }
bigNumber => !int8 ^ { min: -500  max: 5000 }
          →  !numeric.integer_type { size: { bits: 8  signed: true }  min: -500  max: 5000 }
```

`bigNumber` retargets to `numeric.integer_type` (an instance's tag is always the base constructor) and keeps `int8`'s `size` binding, which nothing in its own body mentions — replacement rather than merge would silently discard the width and yield an unconstrained-width integer with bounds. Recognition is syntactic — the `^` declares the intent — and the resolver verifies it: the target MUST resolve to a value whose type states refinement rules, and the retarget follows the value's tag. The result carries `I`'s constructor as its tag and `I`, with everything `I` IS-A, in `chain.supertypes` — `[int8 integer]` for `bigNumber`.

**End state.** After desugaring, every *closed* non-alias type-def body in resolver output is a binding record `!C { bindings }`, where `C` is the constructor the form applies — with the container constructors parameterless (§4.2), the nearest constructor in the source chain for every container closure is the container constructor itself: `!meta.array` for every array form, `!meta.map` for the map sugar, `!meta.set_type` for an application of `set<T>` — and `bindings` supplies values for the vocabulary's unmarked-name fields, plus the fields the application or refinement bound. Pins, defaults, and routes divide by where their values live: a pin or default whose value is *concrete in the head's own declaration* (`meta.set_type`'s `ordered: = false`) comes from the vocabulary and does not appear in the binding record, while a value routed by a user-template parameter (§5.10) is application-supplied information and does appear — so a validator reads any closed body with a one-hop lookup of the head, never substitution. A type slot is an ordinary required `meta.type_ref`-typed field (§4.2), and the bound name appears in the binding record as a bare token (which, after lifting, is what every closed form holds; §5.3). Binding records are closed-world: they never contain parameter references. A definition whose parameters remain open is either a **record template** — a `!meta.record_template`, read as the vocabulary, a parameter labelled in a field's `type_param` or `value_param` or standing in an application's arguments (§5.10) — or a **held** constructor application, the same `!C { … }` text, unread against `C`'s vocabulary until materialisation substitutes the parameters away (§5.10, §8.2); so constructors carry `!meta.record` vocabulary bodies, record templates carry `!meta.record_template` bodies, every other open entry carries a held application, and every other non-alias entry carries a binding record — or, for a value entry, the value as read against its type (§4.1). The surface abbreviations exist only in source text — except the one that names an alias: a use site names whatever entry the author named, an alias included, and output states the chain as written (§8.3).

**Top-level constructor applications are constructions.** A declaration whose body applies a constructor — the explicit form (`lookup => !meta.map { key_type: text  value_type: integer }`) or its sugar spelling (`lookup => {text => integer}`) — resolves as a construction: kind from the constructor's family, the constructor as the value's tag (§8.1), the binding record as the value, and no supertypes (construction transfers kind, not IS-A). Two such declarations are nominally distinct entries with structurally equal bodies. A declaration whose body is a fully-bound application of a non-constructor *template* resolves on the same footing: the declaration **is** the entry — the substituted binding record its value, closed in place, the canonical application recorded as `@meta.application` on its key — and no parallel entry is minted beside it (§5.10, §8.2). Two such declarations are two entries, as two hand-written records with one field list are.

**Named definitions required.** The refinement form (`!I ^ { values }`) and constructor application (`!C value`) are valid only as the top-level body of a declaration — the inline prohibition of §5.2. A constrained atom must be introduced with its own declaration and referenced by name.


### 5.7 Refinement

Refinement copies an existing value and tightens it — binding values, fixing defaults, restricting ranges — producing a new definition with its own identity that IS-A the source. It never changes the field set: no field is added and none removed. It is expressed with the `^` operator between a source and a body, and **a value is refined under the rules its type states** (§5.5): a record under `meta.record`'s rules, which this section states; an atom instance under its constructor's facet rules (below); the kernel's reference and map instances under theirs. A type that states no rule for a field the body names refuses the refinement at `^`, naming the type, so the rule is total.

```
config => { host: text  port?: integer ~ 8080  debug: boolean }
production => config ^ { host?: = "prod.example.com"  port?: = 9090 }
```

The operator carries the operation: `^` always means *refine, preserving IS-A*, at every level — record types (`config ^ { ... }`), constructors (`meta.array ^ { ... }`, a constructor being a record, §4.1), and atom and other instances (`!integer ^ { ... }`, `!meta.type_ref ^ { … }`, §5.5). A source type name followed directly by a braced body, with no operator, is a parse error; the diagnostic SHOULD suggest `^` (refinement) or `&` (composition). A refinement head admits no removal clause: `T ^ { ... } - { ... }` is a parse error — an operator that promises IS-A cannot host the operation that revokes it (§5.9).

In a refinement, only existing fields may be modified: fields in the body MUST exist in the source definition, and adding fields is a resolver error. The guiding rule is that refinement can only restrict, never expand. The source name resolves in the document's namespace; the same syntax refines local and imported types. A refinement whose source is a FINAL record is a resolver error (§5.2).

**Field facts move along three orders, and never backwards.** A field's three questions (§5.2) each carry an order from least to most determined, and a restatement refines its source exactly when no question moves backwards:

- **omission** — *nothing* → *required* → *injected*: what a missing key yields, read off the declaration as `?` without a value, an unmarked name, and `?` with a value;
- **voidable** — *true* → *false*;
- **role** — `FREE` → `DEFAULT` → `FIXED`, a default overridable until it is fixed.

So `a: T` → `a?: T ~ v` (required → injected, FREE → DEFAULT) is allowed; `a?: T ~ v` → `a: T` is refused, a default not being removable; `a?: T ~ v` → `a?: T = v` is allowed; `a?: T?` → `a?: T` (voidable true → false) and `a?: T` → `a: T` (nothing → required) are allowed, and each reverse is refused. The third of §5.2's invariants shapes one move: `a?: T? ~ v` reaches a pin only as `a?: T = v`, voidable and role moving forward together, since a pin on a voidable type is refused. One consequence is worth stating: a marker written in a base, `a: T = v`, may be relaxed by a subtype to `a?: T = v` — required → injected — exactly as `a: T` may be relaxed to `a?: T ~ v`; a family that needs its marker written in every member restates it unmarked in each. Nothing stores the omission answer, and nothing needs to: the order is checked on the derived answer, which is `optional` and `value` read together (§8.1). These orders are `meta.record`'s rules and hold for every record, a constructor among them: a constructor refinement (`meta.set_type => meta.array ^ { ordered?: = false }`) moves a default to a pin or a required field to an injected one as any refinement does, and never replaces a pin.

**A restated field's type narrows by IS-A.** A restated field's type MUST be the inherited type, or a type IS-A it (§7.2) — alias hops followed on both sides, judged at link where every name both types hold resolves — or `void` at a voidable position; any other restatement is a resolver error against the restating declaration, in refinement and composition alike (§5.8's tightening entries follow the same rule). The relation is nominal because it is the one every typed position already admits by, so a value of the refinement read at the source's position meets the field's type by the rule that reads it, and because it is total and decidable from names, where value-set inclusion would need a containment judgement for every pair of families that the series states nowhere. So `[int8]` restating `[int32]`, a variant restating its choice, and a fresh `!numeric.integer_type { size: { bits: 8 } }` restating `int32` each narrow a value set and are refused, no IS-A edge joining either pair; the kernel's `meta.namespace => meta.map ^ { key_type: meta.identifier_ref … }` passes by `meta.identifier_ref`'s refinement of `meta.type_ref`. A structural narrowing between containers is a rule of its own for each container constructor to state.

**Restatement.** Each field may be restated as itself. A restatement may change a **default** (defaults are overridable, §5.2) and MUST NOT change a **pin** — the identity case exists so a body may restate a fixed field without error, not so fixed values can be replaced. This is what forces a discriminated family's arrangement (§5.2): the base cannot pin a selector, since no member could then pin its own. A dependent field (§5.2) is restated only as itself: a refinement may make it required, and `v?: int32` over `v?: kind!` is refused, as is a typed field restated as a dependent one; pinning its sibling is what fixes its type, and the materialised body keeps `type: kind!` beside the pin.

**Value tightening is per facet kind.** "Refinement can only restrict" is checkable per constraint field, and each field's rule follows its facet's kind, declared once here for every family: an **ordered bound** (`min`, `max`, `min_length`, `max_items`, exclusive bounds, and kin) may move only inward — a lower bound may rise, an upper bound may fall, never the reverse — and requires its family's value space to be **ordered**, which every family carrying one is (§5.5; the temporal split is what made `duration`'s so, and a float's bounds judge the ordered values, the infinities among them, and exclude NaN, which no interval contains); a **step** (`multiple_of`, wherever it appears) is strictly positive, tests the value's magnitude with the sign ignored, and may tighten only to an integer multiple of the inherited step (`15` under `5` tightens; `10` under `15` is an error); a **permission** facet (a boolean granting latitude: `numeric.float_type`'s `allow_nan` and its three companions, the `allow_relative` and `allow_fragment` of `net.uri_type` and `net.iri_type`, `net.hostname_type`'s `allow_idn`, `net.media_type_type`'s `allow_parameters`, and `meta.reference_type`'s `allow_sibling`) may go from granted to withdrawn, never the reverse; a **member set** (an enum's `members`, the `members` of the exact numeric tiers, the `schemes` of a URI or IRI family, the `types` and `suffixes` of a media-type family) may shrink to a subset, never grow or replace, compared by the family's own value identity (§5.5, §7.4); a **settable-once** facet (`unicode.text_type.pattern` and `unicode.text_type.members`) may be set by a refinement where the source left it unset, restated verbatim, or left alone, and never changed — the two text facets occupy one logical position, both specifying the admitted value set, and `pattern` cannot be narrowed without a regular-language containment oracle the series decides nowhere, so giving the pair two rules by which spelling the author reached for would make the narrowing relation an artifact of notation; that `unicode.text_type.members` is settable-once where the numeric tiers' member sets shrink is the one departure from the member-set rule, stated so that a reader does not infer it from a diagnostic; a facet **fixed at construction** is pinned where it is constructed — a refinement of the *constructor* may pin it, which is how `enum` states its `name_type` — and a refinement of an *instance* restates it verbatim or leaves it alone, and never sets or moves it, because moving it would change what a token *means* rather than narrow which values are admitted — `unicode.text_type.normalization` (turning a fold on in a refinement re-reads every token the source admitted); the profile facets of `unicode.identifier_type`, `start`, `continue`, `start_add`, `continue_add`, `medial` and `exclude`, for which settable-once would be unsound, since setting `start_add` where the source left it unset *widens* the profile (`!meta.identifier ^ { start_add: "_" }` would admit `_x` without `_x` being an identifier); and an enum's or a record's `name_type` (§5.2, §7.4) — so that a different form, profile or name type is a fresh construction and never a refinement; a **selector** facet — a discriminant among members of a closed vocabulary (`numeric.integer_type.size`, `numeric.complex_type.component`, `binary.bytes_type.encoding`, `numeric.float_type.format`) — may move under refinement **only along the narrowing relation its members carry**, which each family states: a width chain for `size` (`int8` is inside `int16`, so `!int8 ^ { size: { bits: 16 } }` widens and fails, whatever the arithmetic direction); a partial order for `component` (`INTEGER ⊂ NUMBER ⊂ RATIONAL` and `FLOAT32 ⊂ FLOAT64`, the exact and approximate families incomparable since binary64 carries ±inf and NaN that no exact decimal represents, so `!complex ^ { component: INTEGER }` narrows and `!complex ^ { component: FLOAT64 }` is refused, a float complex being its own instance); and **no relation at all** for `encoding`, which a refinement may neither set nor change — an alphabet is a spelling of one value space, so a change would produce an IS-A carrying no narrowing, and another alphabet is another instance of the constructor (§5.5); a **reference's `of`** narrows to a type IS-A the source's, so every name the refinement admits the source admits too (§4.2). A selector at the constructor's default is not thereby free: the relation is over effective values, so an unwritten default and a written one refine identically, and there is no "set-from-default" permission apart from the relation. A **fixed** value follows §5.2's rules for a pin, and a written value is compared against it as a value (§5.5). A refinement value violating its facet's rule is a resolver error at schema load, in record refinement and value refinement alike (§5.5, §5.6).

**Three ways to name a type after another, and what each buys.** An author writing `user_id => uuid` and expecting a `stock_id` to be refused at a `user_id` position gets no protection, and nothing else tells them the other two forms exist:

| Spelling | Relation to `uuid` | `!uuid` at its position | a peer's tag |
|---|---|---|---|
| `user_id => uuid` — **alias** (§8.3) | the same type, under another name | accepted | accepted |
| `user_id => !uuid ^ {}` — **refinement** | IS-A `uuid`; usable at a `uuid` position | refused | refused |
| `user_id => !net.uuid_type {}` — **fresh instance** (§5.5) | unrelated in either direction | refused | refused |

Only the first is a rename, and it is what identity dereferences (§8.2): `box<user_id>` and `box<uuid>` are one application. The **empty refinement is legal** — `!uuid ^ {}` narrows nothing and is the language's one nominal-subtype spelling, which is why "a refinement tightens" is a description of what a refinement may do and not a MUST that a body change something.

**Open modifiers.** In a record template body (§5.10) the value of a `=` or `~` modifier may be a parameter: `bounded => <N> { attempts?: integer = N }` routes, and `retry_policy => <N> { attempts?: integer ~ N }` defaults. **The name's mark is the author's, exactly as beside a literal.** The `?` on a name says whether the key may be missing and says nothing about the value (§5.2), so it does not wait for the value to exist: `a?: T ~ P` is a default, `a?: T = P` an injected pin, and `a: T = P` a marker every document must write — and `a: T ~ P` is refused for the reason `a: T ~ v` is, a default on a key that is always written being a value nothing can reach. What the declaration does not settle is the *value*: it arrives at application, and every application MUST bind every parameter (§5.10), so it is always supplied. While the entry is open the parameter is the field's `value_param` (§5.10, §8.1), labelled so that no literal spelled like a parameter is mistaken for one. **The role is the modifier's, as beside a literal**: `= P` is `FIXED` and `~ P` `DEFAULT` where they are written, and materialisation substitutes the argument into `value` without changing a fact — so the materialised entry enforces what the literal form `status?: int32 = 201` one line down would (a document writing `status: 999` is a validation error, and an omitted `status` injects `201`), and the bound value is checked against the field's type by the value-conformance rule (§5.2). A FREE field never carries a value, open or closed. In a *fresh* record template a field pinned to a value parameter is a **selector** whatever its name's mark (§5.10): one value per application is one pin per member, the base reading the field required with the pin dropped and each member FIXED to its argument, optional as the name was marked. A literal value needs a closed type: a field whose type is a parameter, or an application over one, takes a parameter or no value (§5.10). In a *record* template body the modifier spellings are the only way to bind a field's value to a parameter — `min_items: S` in a record body is a field declaration whose type is `S`, not a value binding; in a held *constructor* body (`<S> !meta.array { element_type: text  min_items: S }`) the slot is a value slot of `meta.array`'s own vocabulary and the parameter stands in it directly (§5.10).

**Elided type expressions.** In a refinement or supertype-composition body, the type expression in a field definition MAY be elided: when only a modifier is present (`field: = value` or `field: ~ value`), the field's type is inherited from the source declaration and only the value state changes. A modifier-only entry is always a tightening — it names no type, so it cannot declare a new field — and a modifier-only entry whose name matches no inherited field is a resolver error. Restating the type expression remains necessary when the tightening also narrows the field's type. In a fresh record definition there is no inherited declaration to elide toward: every field MUST have an explicit type expression, and the resolver MUST reject modifier-only entries there.

**Refinement requires a value its type states rules for** (§4.3 states the rule for both operator families). The source of `^` — after following its alias chain (§8.3) — MUST be a record (a fresh or refined record, a composition, a constructor, or an *applied* record template — a record template named bare at an operand is a resolver error, §5.10), or an instance whose constructor states facet rules. A container instance (`lookup => {text => integer}`) is **finished**: its bindings are set, `meta.map` states no tightening rule for them, and `^` on it is a resolver error; a choice is likewise inadmissible (§4.3). A record instantiation is not finished: it closes to a `!meta.record` with fields, and whether its source was written as `box<text>` inline or as a name for that application is provenance, which never gates an operator (§4.3). There is no refinement of an application head: `{text => text} ^ { min_items: 1 }` has no head to refine, and the use case is the size specifier on the sugar itself (`{text => text; 1..}`, §5.3). Record refinement chains remain open because record refinement re-emits a `!meta.record` body; a declared construction (`lookup => {text => integer}`) is terminal — authors wanting a narrower relative write the bounds on the form (`strict_lookup => {text => integer; 1..}`). The `refined-def` grammar's optional `<type-args>` head serves user-template heads only (§5.10). Value refinement (`!I ^`, §5.5) is a distinct form with its own body grammar and the same rule.

A refined record definition remains a definition: it can be refined further or instantiated. A refinement that narrows a field's type to `void` — `a?: void?`, or `a: void?` at a restated group member (§5.11) — forbids the field's value in the refined type while keeping the field in the contract, the IS-A-preserving counterpart of removal (§5.9); `void` refines every type at a voidable position, the value set `{_}` being a subset of every voidable position's. Individual map entries cannot be refined because map keys are data, not definition fields.

**Body materialisation.** The refined body re-emits the complete inherited field set in source order; each field carries either its inherited state and value or the tightened ones. The materialised body is self-describing — consumers of resolver output do not walk the supertype chain to learn the field set. Inherited defaults and pins appear with their values even when the refinement did not refer to them.

**Resolution.** The refined entry's `chain.supertypes` records the source and the IS-A chain through it (§8.1), its value carries the source's constructor as its tag, and tightened fields appear in the materialised body. A refinement keeps its source's `name_type` (§5.2).


### 5.8 Supertype Composition

Supertype composition (`&`) is a construction tool: it combines one or more parent types with new fields into a new definition, declaring an IS-A relationship with each listed supertype. New fields are permitted; existing fields may be tightened.

```
address => { street: text  city: text  postcode: text }
contact => { name: text  email: text }
customer => address & contact & { loyalty_tier: text }
```

**Supertype field conflicts.** The supertypes MUST contribute disjoint field sets — a field name appearing in more than one supertype path is a resolver error, including diamond cases where the field traces to the same originating type through both paths. Two names are one field under the composed record's name type (§5.2), and **supertypes with different name types do not compose**: `json_record & address`, where `address` states nothing and so is `meta.field_name`'s, is a resolver error, and the record to compose is one declared over the same type.

**The trailing body.** The trailing `& { ... }` body is optional (`customer => address & contact` is valid). Body fields that match an inherited field are tightening entries and follow the refinement rules of §5.7 (including elided type expressions and the IS-A narrowing of a restated type); body fields that match no inherited field are new fields.

**Field ordering.** Supertypes contribute fields in left-to-right order as listed; each supertype's fields appear in their declared order. Tightening entries replace inherited fields in place; new fields are appended after all inherited fields. This is what lets a base's sibling precede a subtype's dependent field (§5.2), and a sibling may be a discriminator: the family rules ask of it what they ask of any required atom-typed field.

**Constructorness is inherited through composition.** Composition propagates the supertype chain, so an entry composing with something IS-A `meta.top` is IS-A `meta.top` and is therefore a constructor (§4.2): `net.iri_type => unicode.text_type & atom.specification & { ... }` is a constructor because `unicode.text_type` is. A record mixin such as `atom.specification` lends vocabulary without conferring the level, and a composition reaching no base kind — `box => <T> base & { value: T }` over a plain record `base` — is an ordinary template, not a constructor, whatever its author intended.

```
atom.specification => { spec: iri }
net.iri_type => unicode.text_type & atom.specification & {
  spec?:           = "https://www.rfc-editor.org/rfc/rfc3987"
  schemes?:        net.scheme_set
  allow_relative?: boolean ~ true
  allow_fragment?: boolean ~ true
  normalization?:  = NONE
}
```

`net.iri_type` is a constructor, IS-A `unicode.text_type` and `atom.specification` directly. Its fields, in order: `unicode.text_type`'s six constraint fields, the last of them — `normalization` — tightened in place from a default to the pin `NONE`; `atom.specification`'s `spec`, tightened in place to an injected pin via an elided-type modifier; and the three new fields. Composition onto a FINAL record is a resolver error (§5.2), and a supertype that is ABSTRACT confers nothing on the composed entry, which states its own extension and is OPEN unless it says otherwise. A restated field that is a **group member** stays a member, in its own option: a `?` on its name is the in-option mark, which a restatement may drop and may not add (§5.11). A declared application of a template whose body is not a record (`string_triple => vector<text, 3>`) closes to a binding record and has no vocabulary body to compose with; a record template's declared application is a `!meta.record`, which composes like any other (§4.3, §5.7).

**A restated field's annotations are the restatement's own followed by the inherited ones.** §6 puts an annotation on a `meta.record_field` and §8.1 preserves it; composition copies inherited fields whole and §5.7 lets a body entry restate one, and the two paths must agree. The rule: a restated field — in a composition body or a refinement body, including §5.7's modifier-only entry — carries **the restatement's own annotations, in source order, followed by the inherited field's, in source order**. Nothing is dropped and no name is treated as a key, because [TSON-DATA] §3.1 already makes an annotation name repeatable on one value and annotations are a list; and the restatement leads because order is already the precedence mechanism — every first-occurrence lookup reads it that way, so the nearer declaration wins at every such site without any of them knowing about composition. A restatement adds and never removes: a subtype rewriting an inherited `@doc` leaves the field carrying both, which is what "annotations are not removable" buys. Per-name replacement would need a cardinality on the declared annotation, which v1 does not have. The rule is what makes a count along a composed chain checkable, and it has read-side force wherever an annotation directs reading: a field that tightens presence and writes no annotation of its own inherits its source's, rather than silently losing it. It also fixes which fields carry a selector over a chain (§5.2): `meta.record.discriminators` is the base's statement and the member restates the field pinned, so no per-field mark travels and none has to be cleared.

**Parameterized operands.** Both `&` composition and `^` refinement operate on type references, which may carry arguments. A refinement of a parameterized type must re-declare its open parameters in its own `<>` slot (§5.10); composing with a parameterized supertype works the same way: `ok => <T> result<T> & { note: text }`. An operand may carry arguments, which may name the declaration's parameters; **an operand that is itself a parameter is a resolver error** — `<T> T & { name: text }` and `<T> T ^ { … }` are refused under the shadowing rule (§5.10), since a parameter has no field set to flatten until the template is applied, and a same-named entry in the schema's namespace is that rule's to refuse. A record template's `chain.parents` is a list of `meta.type_ref` values, which may carry arguments, because a parent may be written as an application: while the composing declaration is open, `result<T>` stands there with its arguments, and is substituted and closed with every other reference the record holds (§5.10) — so `ok<text>` reaches an IS-A edge to `result<text>` and not to `result<int32>`. The closed chain `supertypes` records names without arguments, being computed once every parent is a type; the closed parent's name and its own chain are folded into it when the instantiation closes. A closed type keeps no `parents` and carries no arguments in its chain, so a schema with no open parent produces a chain of bare names.

**An application at an operand is subsumed where it stands, and reaches the head.** An application standing at a composition operand is absorbed by value: its arguments are the member's own contribution (`dog => pet<"dog", dog_type> & { … }` is the hand-written `dog => pet & { pet_type?: = "dog"  pet: dog_type }` with one IS-A edge to `pet`), and it mints no entry (§5.10). The edge reaches the **head** — the template — because the member is a declared member of the template's family; and it reaches the entry the arguments name where one exists. **A declared application of a template whose parent is an application of another record template is IS-A that template too**: `ok_of => ok<text>` over `ok => <T> result<T> & { note: text }` is IS-A `ok` and `result<text>` and `result`, as its hand-written expansion `result<text> & { note: text }` would be — the application and its expansion land in one family, and a position typed `result` admits both. Joining the outer family brings its obligation: the member must pin the outer family's discriminators, which `ok`'s own pins, or its arguments, supply. A parent application nothing declares gives a *minted* record no chain edge (§5.2, §8.2). A record template named *without* arguments at an operand is a resolver error: a position may name the base, but an operand is flattened, and a template has fields only once its parameters are bound (§5.10).

**Resolution.** The composed entry's `chain.supertypes` records the listed parents and, transitively, their own chains (§8.1), complete to a fixed point; inherited fields are copied into the body's `fields` map in the order above, so the body is flat and a consumer never repeats the merge. Which operands were written is not recorded for a closed record: direct parents are the chain's minimal members, and operand order is in the flattened `fields`. §5.8's one name type over a composition's supertypes is judged at link, once every entry is ready (§3.4.1).


### 5.9 Subtraction

Subtraction removes fields from a construction and deliberately breaks IS-A — the resulting type is no longer source-compatible. Taxonomically it is a construction operation, not a refinement: like composition, it computes a new field set; unlike composition, it disclaims the contract. It is expressed as a trailing **removal clause** on a construction head:

```
removal-set = "-" ws "{" ws field-name *( separator field-name ) ws "}"
```

```
account => { name: text  email: text  password: text }
account_public => account - { password }
account_view   => account & { email?: text ~ "n/a" } - { password }
staff_public   => account & user & { badge: text } - { password  ssn }
```

The clause is head-level: a reader of the declaration line knows the contract is broken without scanning the body. It attaches only to construction heads — a bare source, or an `&` chain with or without a trailing body; a refinement head admits no removal clause (§5.7, §12.1).

Rules:

1. **Resolution order.** Supertypes merge first — §5.8's disjointness rule fires here unchanged, so subtraction cannot be used to resolve diamond conflicts — the body applies second, and removals apply last.
2. **Removing a nonexistent field is a resolver error** — symmetric with refinement's existing-fields-only rule.
3. **Source path does not restrict removal.** Removal operates on the merged field set, not on field provenance; since IS-A is already broken, there is no contract to violate.
4. **The body and the removal set are disjoint.** A removal naming a field the body itself introduces is a resolver error — adding and removing a field in one declaration is incoherent — and a body entry tightening a removed field is a resolver error. Body entries tightening the remaining inherited fields follow §5.7's rules.
5. **Removal and narrowing to `void` are distinct.** `- { field }` removes the field from the contract and breaks IS-A; `field?: void?` (§5.2, §5.7) keeps the field in the contract, forbids its value, and preserves IS-A.
6. **Empty subtraction does not exist**, by grammar: the removal set requires at least one name, and `source & {}` is composition-with-no-additions and preserves IS-A.
7. **Groups.** A removal may name a field-group member; the member leaves its option, an emptied option leaves the group, and a group the declaration rules would then refuse is rewritten as the spelling it equals (§5.11). A removal naming a dependent field's sibling removes the dependency's ground: the dependent field is removed with it or the declaration is a resolver error.
8. **Constructors and parameters.** A subtracted construction whose head is a constructor is not one — it has no supertypes, so it is IS-A nothing and may not be applied — and a parameterised subtraction declares a record template whose field set is the merged set minus the removals, with no parents.
9. **FINAL does not reach subtraction.** A removal off a FINAL record is admissible: FINAL constrains the IS-A set (§5.2), which subtraction never joins, so the result is an unrelated type that happens to share a field list. Composition and refinement, which both mint an IS-A edge, are the two that FINAL refuses.

**Resolution.** A subtracted record is a record with no supertypes: `chain.supertypes` is empty — the subtracted type is not source-compatible — and nothing records what it was subtracted from, provenance being what was written and the source document having it (§4.1). A removed field's annotations are lost with the field. When a resolved form is read (§8.1), the empty `supertypes` is read as stated rather than inferred by diffing field sets against the parents.


### 5.10 Templates and Parameters

A type definition may declare parameters using `<>` immediately after `=>`. Parameters are local names referenced in the body and bound to concrete arguments when the definition is referenced with `<args>`. Each parameter has a type, derived from how the body uses it, and may be written with one — `<T: text, N: int8>` (below). A definition with parameters is a **template** — it cannot be instantiated directly, and references to it MUST supply arguments for all its parameters:

```
container => <T> { items: [T] }
pair      => <T, U> { first: T  second: U }
vector    => <T, N> !meta.array { element_type: T  min_items: N  max_items: N }
matrix    => <T, M, N> [[T; N]; M]
```

`container<text>`, `vector<pixel, 1920>`, and `matrix<pixel, 1080, 1920>` are concrete types; bare `container` or `vector` references are resolver errors, except that a record template may be named bare as a family base (below). A template application's head resolves per §3.3.2; parameters and templates are an authoring-side feature exclusively — resolved output governing data carries none of it at any closed position (§1.3, §8.2).

**Template forms, and the two kinds of template.** A template declaration's body may be a record (`container`), a container or sugar form (`matrix`), a reference (a pure application body, below), or — the fourth form — an **instance**: a constructor application whose bindings mention parameters (`vector` above). The instance form is the route to a constructor with no sugar of its own (`bounded_set => <N> !meta.set_type { element_type: text  min_items: N }`) and the target the sugar desugars into; for the sugared constructors the compact spelling exists alongside it (`vector` could equally be written `<T, N> [T; N]`). The forms divide in two, and the division is the one C++ (alias template / class template), Haskell (`type` / `data`) and ML (abbreviation / `datatype`) all make. A **record template** — a fresh record, a composition or a refinement whose body is a record — is a *family*: a type a position may name, whose open form is a base and whose applications are its members (below). Every other template — over a container, an atom constructor, a choice, or an alias — is an *abbreviation*: its open form denotes nothing, and an application of it is its expansion (§8.2). The kernel states the split with two constructors. A record template is a **`meta.record_template`**: an ordinary record body read as the vocabulary, over `parameters: [meta.template_param; 1..]`, whose fields are `meta.record_template_field`s — a record field's four facts, where the type and the value may each be a parameter of the enclosing template, and the label says which:

```
record_template_field => {
  ( type: meta.field_type_ref | type_param: meta.param_name )
  optional?: boolean ~ false
  voidable?: boolean ~ false
  role?:     meta.field_role ~ FREE
  ( value: type! | value_param: meta.param_name )?
}
```

`type_param` names a parameter standing for the whole type, `value_param` one standing for the whole value; `type` and `value` are as on a record field. Every other open entry is a **`meta.template`** — `template => meta.top & { parameters: [meta.template_param]  template: text }` — the constructor application as written, held as text and *unread* until materialisation substitutes its parameters away. One substitution rule serves both (below). A record is closed by construction — no field of a `meta.record` carries a parameter — which is why the family base is its own constructor and not a record with an optional list of parameters: a reader, a writer or a member dispatcher built from a record cannot be handed a template, and a consumer of resolved output tells the two apart by the tag (§1.3).

**Held bodies, and where a parameter stands.** A `meta.template`'s body is not a typed quotation of the constructor's vocabulary, and it is not read against that vocabulary while a parameter remains: a sugar form is desugared per §5.3's table and held; an explicit `<T, N> !meta.array { … }` is held as written. Because a held body is not classified by slot kind, a parameter needs no channel of its own: a parameter in a value slot (`min_items: N`), in a type slot (`element_type: T`), inside a collection (`variants: [T error]`, `members: [a b M]`), or at any depth is a token like any other, and **collection-valued slots are parameterizable** — `result => <T> ( T | error )`, the sum-typed result envelope, resolves and closes to an ordinary `meta.choice` body. A **record template is not held**: `<T> { x: T }` is `!meta.record_template { fields: { x => !meta.record_template_field { type_param: T } }  parameters: [{ name: T  type: meta.type_ref }] }`, read as the vocabulary, and a composition or refinement template is resolved against its namespace first — a tightening entry states a modifier and no type expression, so it is not a field until the inherited field supplies one — and the *flattened* record is the entry, its `chain.parents` the parents as written (§5.8). In a record template a parameter stands in **three places and no other**: a field's whole type (`v: T`, labelled `type_param`), a field's whole default or fixed value (`w?: int32 ~ N`, `type: text = N`, labelled `value_param`), or an argument of an application in a field's type or in a parent (`items: box<T>`, `result<T> & { … }` — sugar inside a field, `[T; N]`, lifts to an application and so is the third), where it is recognised by its text resolving into the template's `parameters` (the shadowing rule, below) — the one place resolved output still applies that rule. A parameter anywhere else in a record body — as a field name, a group member, `optional`, `voidable` or `role`, `extension`, `name_type` or `discriminators` — is a resolver error at the declaration naming the slot, since the family base (below) would then have no shape its members share. A parameter is never an operand of `&` or `^` (§5.8). The labels are the resolver's: a token the template's own body writes is a parameter by the shadowing rule, and is labelled where the body is normalised, its parameters in scope; nothing is classified after that. **A field the template takes from a closed source keeps its literal**, even one spelled like the template's parameter (`base => { kind?: text = T }` with `fam => <T> base & { v: T }`: `fam`'s `kind` is `value: T`, not `value_param: T`), so "a literal spelled like a live parameter is unreachable" reaches only what the template writes. `!meta.record_template { … }` written in source is refused as resolver vocabulary, as `!meta.template { … }` is.

**A literal value needs a closed type.** A field whose type is a parameter, or an application over one, takes `value_param` or no value: `<T> { v?: T ~ 5 }` and `<M> { v?: capped<M> ~ 5 }` are refused at the declaration, where judging the literal per application would make a template valid for some arguments and not others; the rule makes the template's verdict its own. The same rule reaches a literal *argument*: with `pin2 => <T, P> { a?: T = P }`, `<X> pin2<X, 3> & { … }` and `<X> { f: pin2<X, 3> }` bind `P`, a value of `T`'s argument, to `3` while that argument is the open `X`, and both are refused at the application, naming `3`, `P` and `X`; `pin2<int8, 3>` is a closed application and its member pins `a` to `3`. A literal under a closed type is held as written and judged when the template closes, against the closed field's type (§5.2); the kernel states the rule structurally, `value` being typed `type!` and having no sibling beside `type_param` (§5.2, §8.1).

**Substitution is one rule at any depth.** Binding a template's parameters rewrites the body — the held text, or the record template's fields and parents — by replacing every token whose text resolves into the entry's parameter list with the corresponding argument — a reference argument as a reference (a bare name, or an application where the argument is itself one), a literal argument as written — and nothing else is classified. An argument is **read as its parameter's recorded type** (below) and then substituted: where that type is `meta.type_ref` it must resolve as a type, where it is a value type it must be a value of it, and in an enum's member list it is a member — `e => <M> !enum [a b M]` applied as `e<c>` admits `c` because `M` is recorded as a text value, not because of how `c` is spelled. When every parameter of an entry closes, a held body is read, once, against the constructor's own vocabulary — materialisation binds against vocabulary exactly once, at the only moment binding is decidable — and a record template's fields take their arguments in place, `type_param` becoming `type` and `value_param` becoming `value`, carrying the argument, with no other fact changed; the result is an ordinary closed body, a `!meta.record` for a record template (§8.2). A value where a type parameter stands (`box<3>`) and an application where a value parameter stands are refused at the application, by the label the argument lands at.

**One spelling.** A held body is carried as text and compared as the **parsed form** — never as the text, and never as bound values — and an open synthetic's identity and content-derived name are computed over that parsed form with the parameters renamed positionally (§8.2), so the open form MUST have **one spelling** however many phases produce it: no-argument type names as bare tokens, applications as references with their argument lists, and parameter tokens unquoted — a producer that quotes every token has written a body that references no parameters at all. Whitespace is free: comparing the parsed form is what keeps "one spelling" from becoming a canonical-whitespace requirement the series has no emitter contract to support. Two of the template shapes cannot be normalised syntactically (composition and refinement need a namespace to flatten against), so "one phase" is not achievable and "one spelling" is the requirement.

**A parameter's type is derived from the positions it stands in, and recorded.** Every parameter of an open entry carries the type an argument for it is read as — the kernel's `meta.template_param => { name: meta.param_name  type: meta.type_ref  bound?: meta.type_ref }` (§8.1) — and the type is *derived*, never required of the author: it is the declared type of the slot the parameter stands in, in the vocabulary of the constructor the body applies. No parameter lacks one, since a parameter the body never references is refused (below) and every position has a declared type:

- **A type slot** — a field declared `meta.type_ref`, such as `meta.array.element_type`, `meta.map.key_type` or a record field's type — gives `meta.type_ref`.
- **A value slot** gives the slot's declared type: `min_items: N` gives `numeric.count`, and an enum's member list gives `text`.
- **A routed default or fixed value** (`w?: int32 ~ N`) gives the *field's* declared type, `int32` — §5.2's value conformance, read off the field. Where the field is itself typed by a parameter, `box => <T, N> { w?: T ~ N }`, the derived type is that parameter: `N`'s `type` is `T`. That is the one scoping rule a parameter list needs: **a `type` may name a parameter of the same template declared before it**, and nothing else in the schema's namespace under that name; at an application the argument for `N` is then read as the type `T`'s argument names.
- **An argument to another template** gives that template's recorded type for the position, so derivation is a **fixed point** across templates that apply each other. A parameter the fixed point leaves undetermined is given `meta.type_ref`: an alias template's body *is* its application (`meta.alias.target` is a reference, §8.1), so `loop => <T> loop<T>` has no slot for a concrete use and its `T` is a type parameter — what is wrong with that declaration is that it applies itself forever and denotes no type, and that is what its diagnostic names.
- **A payload in §5.6's positional form** is walked as the one field it binds: `names => <M> !enum [a b M]` puts `M` in `meta.enum_type.members` exactly as `!enum { members: [a b M] }` does. The kernel's own `meta.ref => <T> !meta.reference_type { of: T  allow_sibling: false }` records `T` as `meta.type_ref`, `of`'s declared type, so it is a type parameter whose argument may name a constructor or a type (§4.2).

**The kind follows from the type.** A parameter whose `type` is `meta.type_ref` is a **type parameter**; any other is a **value parameter**. Nothing records the kind apart from the type, since a separate field would state one fact twice. Value parameters bind **scalars only** — numbers, booleans, identifiers, and quoted strings; type parameters bind references, simple or applied. There is no parameter arithmetic: every value argument is used as given.

**Several uses must agree.** A parameter's `type` is the use type that IS-A every other; where the use types are not ordered by IS-A the declaration is a resolver error. `<N> !meta.array { min_items: N  max_items: N }` derives `numeric.count` twice and is fine; `both => <T> { a: T  b?: int32 ~ T }` derives `meta.type_ref` and `int32`, which no argument could satisfy, and is refused — a parameter standing in both kinds of position is this rule's commonest case and needs none of its own. The rule follows IS-A edges only and proves nothing about value sets: two refinements of one source such as `int8` and `int32` are refused together though a small integer satisfies both, and the author declares the narrower type by name and uses it in both places. That is the price of a total, two-valued rule checked once at the declaration, where a list of use types checked one by one would move the verdict to every application. **A declared parameter the body never references is a resolver error** — `<T> !meta.array { element_type: text }` is a mistake, not a degenerate template, and the rule holds for every template form (`box => <T> { v: text }` likewise).

**A declaration may narrow what it derives.** A parameter may be written with a type after its name — `<T: text, N: int8>`, one production for every template form (§12.1) — and the written type is read by the kind the positions give, never the reverse, so an annotation cannot contradict the uses:

- **On a value parameter it narrows `type`.** `<N: int8> { w?: integer ~ N }` records `type: int8`, and the written type MUST be IS-A the type the uses derive, or the declaration is a resolver error: `<N: text> !meta.array { element_type: text  min_items: N }` is refused, `text` not being a `numeric.count`, and so is `<N: int8>` over a `count`-typed slot, `int8` being IS-A `integer` and not `numeric.count`.
- **On a type parameter it is `bound`.** `boxed => <T: text> { a: T }` records `type: meta.type_ref  bound: text`, and an argument MUST name `text` or a type that IS-A it, judged after following its alias chain (§8.3); `boxed<int32>` is refused at the application. `bound` is present only where `type` is `meta.type_ref`, and is its own field because a type parameter carries two facts — its argument is a reference, and the reference is restricted — where narrowing `type` to `text` would lose the first and leave `<T: text> { a: T }` and `<M: text> !enum [a b M]` recording alike.
- **An unwritten parameter keeps what it derives**, so a template that writes no types resolves as it always did.

**A bound is inherited** through another template's argument list, as a type is: `rebox => <T> boxed<T>` records its `T` with `bound: text`, so `rebox<int32>` is refused at its own application rather than deep inside `boxed`. Several inherited bounds agree by the rule for several types, and a written bound MUST be IS-A every inherited one — `<T: currency_code> boxed<T>` narrows it where `currency_code` refines `text`, and `<T: int32> boxed<T>` is refused at the declaration. A bound follows **IS-A edges only**: `date` has a text form without being `text`, and a bound that followed a type's form or its discrimination class would admit it. **A bound names a type, and only a type**: it resolves in the schema's namespace (§3.3.2), and a constructor there — `<T: unicode.text_type>` — admits nothing and is refused at the declaration. Construction transfers kind and no supertypes (§5.5), so a nominal bound cannot say "any text-valued atom": `meta.identifier`, a profile a schema constructs, and `stock_code => !unicode.text_type {}` are IS-A nothing and fail `<T: text>`. That separation is right for values and is a limit for bounds, recorded among the boundaries below.

**A value parameter's type is the slot's declared type, in one namespace.** A value parameter's type is read where the slot it binds into is defined: a constructor's own slot (`meta.array.min_items`, `meta.reference_type.of`) is typed as that constructor declares it, a field the schema types (`w?: integer ~ N`) as the schema declares it, and both resolve in the template's one namespace — the names a constructor's declaration carries being its declaring document's, carried into the closure unchanged (§2.2.3). A written type resolves there too and MUST be IS-A the derived type (above), so `<N: numeric.count>` narrows an `array` bound and `<N: int8>` is refused on one; a schema's own entry named `count` is a different type that never stands in for the kernel's `numeric.count`, two same-named entries being §2.2.3's collision where a closure holds both. **A template whose body applies another template takes that template's form**, following aliases through to the first non-alias head: an alias template (`rb => <N: int8> box<N>` over `box => <N> { w?: integer ~ N }`) takes `box`'s form, reads `int8` against the `integer`-typed default its parameter reaches, and is admitted; `meta.alias` is never itself a form. Two same-named entries with the same resolved body are one type wherever this section compares parameter types — a written type against a derived or inherited one, the several uses of one parameter against each other, and an argument against a parameter's type or bound — and for nothing else: everywhere else a declared entry's identity is its name (§8.2).

**An application is checked at the call site.** Each argument is read as its parameter's `type` before anything is substituted, against the parameter list alone: `vector<pixel, 1920>` reads `pixel` as a type reference and `1920` as a `numeric.count`, and `vector<pixel, "two">` is refused at the argument, not inside the body. A type argument must name a type that IS-A the parameter's bound; a value argument must parse as the parameter's type, or as the type an earlier parameter's argument names. An argument the check cannot judge on its own — an application, a name that does not yet resolve — is left to the substituted body, whose verdict is the same one. The check is what lets a consumer of resolved output — an importing schema, a second resolver comparing output, a host binder generating a generic type — verify an application from the entry, without the held text (§1.3). An argument is a reference or a token by the grammar ([TSON-DATA] §7.4), and its channel is no part of the application: `names<red>` is one application whether `red` was read as a reference or filed as a value at its parameter.

**Scoping and shadowing.** Parameters are local to the declaring definition; they do not escape and do not compose across `&`, and two definitions can independently use the same parameter name. Two positions that must agree share a parameter (`homogeneous_pair => <T> { first: T  second: T }`) — sharing the name is the link. Within a template's body, held or a record template's, parameter names take precedence over the schema's namespace, and **a parameter that shadows a schema entry — a type or a value entry — is a resolver error** — renaming a parameter is free, and silent capture of an entry the body also means to use is the confusability hazard [TSON-DATA] §8.2 exists for. A second refusal keeps the text-recognition of a parameter among an application's arguments unambiguous: **a record template's parameter MUST NOT be spelled like a name or an unquoted token written among the arguments of an application in a field type it absorbs** — from a closed source, or from an open operand other than the operand's own parameters, which the absorber's arguments replace. With `mode => !enum [T U]`, `two => <A, M> { a: A  m?: mode ~ M }` and `pair => <V> { z: two<V, T> }`, `t3 => <T> pair<T> & { x: T }` is refused at `t3`, naming `T` and `z: two<V, T>`, while `pair` itself is valid: without the rule `t3`'s `z` would be `two<T, T>`, its first `T` the parameter and its second the member, which no `meta.record_template_field` can tell apart, and a closed `base => { f: pick<T> }` absorbed into `<T> base & { … }` would make every application's `f` a different type from `base`'s, against IS-A. A literal `value` is not covered: the `value`/`value_param` labels tell it apart. The rule judges the source as it is when absorbed: a closed source of the same schema still writes its applications and is judged; an imported source is materialised, each closed application already the bare name of its entry, so there is nothing to collide with. A template's parameter list is not one of §11.4's hygiene scopes: one author writes it whole on one line, and a deliberate confusable pair there is not a population the list should grow for.

**Declaration-time checking splits by what the parameter list obscures, and nothing more.** Two questions are answered at the declaration from the held body's own field names, needing no stand-in values and so unable to fabricate a verdict: that each binding key names a field the constructor declares (`<T> !meta.array { elemen_type: T }` is a typo, not a template), and that every unmarked-name field is bound. The typing of every *concrete* binding is checked there too (`min_items: "two"` is not a count whatever `T` becomes), as is the unreferenced-parameter rule, from the tokens the held body names, and so are the parameter types themselves: the derivation, the agreement of several uses, and a written type or bound against what the uses derive (above); a literal value's need of a closed type is checked there (above). Everything value-shaped that a parameter obscures waits for materialisation, where the whole body binds through the constructor's own reader (§8.2). An **unapplied** template is checked no further and receives no verdict: a resolver MUST NOT check it by substituting stand-in values, since that manufactures false errors on exactly the slots the mechanism exists for (`<N> !numeric.integer_type { min: N  max: 3 }` is correct for every argument anyone passes and fails under a stand-in of 10). Deferring everything would make the template the one form validated at use rather than at declaration, shipping broken templates to fail at their first user's application site; deferring only what the parameters obscure is the line.

**Where a deferred diagnostic lands.** A materialisation diagnostic MUST be located at the declaration whose text wrote the offending name, with the application as context — deferred checking is survivable only if the author is sent to the line they can edit. The rule is over the *name*, not over the template: `m => <T> !meta.map { key_type: T  value_type: no_such_type }`, a held body whose names are read when it closes, applied by `holder` belongs to `m`, which wrote the name — a record template is read at its declaration, so a name it writes is reported there — while `box => <T> { v: T }` applied as `box<3>` belongs to `holder`, which wrote the `3` — and is found there by the call-site check, before substitution. One defect earns one diagnostic, however many declarations apply the template.

**Sugar inside templates.** A sugar form inside a template body desugars to the same construction it would outside one — §5.3's table unchanged — and lifts to its own synthetic entry, **open** if the form mentions a parameter (§5.3). Nesting needs no special member: an inner form lifts to its own open synthetic, and the outer body holds an ordinary open application of it. At its smallest, `box => <T> { a: [T] }` desugars as if written

```
array_t => <T> !meta.array { element_type: T }
box     => !meta.record_template {
  fields: { a => !meta.record_template_field { type: array_t<T> } }
  parameters: [{ name: T  type: meta.type_ref }]
}
```

with `array_t` an internal name, held, and `box` a record template whose field holds an application still open, its argument a parameter. Applications inside a held body close before its entry is named: desugar lifts innermost-first, so a form it writes already names the entry its inner form became, and a form closed at materialisation MUST agree — `[[pixel; 3]; 3]` written out and `grid<pixel, 3>` closed land on one entry for one type. Because a closed lift hashes its binding record at desugar, before inner applications are rewritten, while an open lift hashes the closed record at materialisation, the two channels can produce two candidate entries for one type (`[box<text>]` written directly, and `[box<T>]` closed with `T := text`); §8.2's merge pass, which re-derives every synthetic's identity from its *resolved* record after Pass 2, is therefore **required**, not an optimisation.

**A record template is a family base, and may be named at a type position.** "A template is not a type" is right for `vector<T, N>` and wrong for a base, and the difference is *elimination*: a position typed `vector` would have to read a value against an element type nothing has fixed, leaving a reader to infer arguments from the payload — ill-defined, since `[]` determines nothing and two instantiations can accept one document. None of that reaches a base, because **no value is ever read against it**: a value there is a value of some member, selected by a tag or by reading the selector fields, and every member is a closed entry with every argument fixed. So a **record template** may stand at a field, element, variant or argument position as a base, and the record template entry *is* the base: a type, built with `meta.record_template` and so admitted by `meta.ref<meta.record_template>` and `meta.type_ref` and by no `meta.ref<meta.record>` (§4.2), what the position names, what `subtypes` indexes, what its own parents' `subtypes` list, and what a host sealed type binds to, with no second entry minted beside it. **Its members are the applications a declaration names** — `bt => box<int32>`, or a composition `dog => pet<"dog", dog_type> & { … }` — and only those. An application written at a use site (`k: box<text>`) is a type, read structurally where it is written, and no member: no declaration names it, so it is never a candidate at a position typed by the template and never needs a name a document cannot write (§8.2). This is §5.2's "a family member is declared", kept by leaving the minted application out rather than by refusing the schema; an author who wants it in the family declares it. **The base's `extension` is ABSTRACT by construction** — never OPEN, nothing being able to write a value whose type is the template rather than one of its applications, and never FINAL, its applications being subtypes by construction — and is not stated: the template's own `extension` is **each application's**, OPEN unless the template says otherwise, which is how a second-level base is spelled, `result => abstract <T> { payload: T }` making `result<text>` abstract over `ok<text>` and `err<text>`; `final` written on a template is a resolver error. Its `discriminators` are the base's selector list: whatever selectors survive erasure of the parameters — a field written `=?`, or, in a *fresh* record template, a field pinned to a value parameter, whatever its name's mark, one value per application being one pin per member (`pet => <N, T> { type?: text = N  pet: T }`); the base reads such a selector required with the pin dropped, and each member is FIXED to its argument, optional as the name was marked (§5.7), exactly as a hand-written member restating `pet_type?: = "dog"` is. `groups` and `name_type` are each application's, unchanged by substitution. A refinement or composition template derives no selector of its own: `<T> meta.array ^ { element_type: = T }` pins a constructor's own facet and states nothing about a family. The one **condition** is on a selector: its declared type MUST mention none of the template's parameters, at any depth — not as its head and not in an argument, a value parameter as much as a type one — because a decoder parses the selectors *before* it knows the member and their pins are exactly what the parameters supply; `type: text = N` satisfies it; over `capped => <M> !numeric.integer_type { max: M }`, `kind: capped<9> = N` satisfies it (a parameter-free application is the entry it expands to, in the base as in every member), and `badge => <M, N> { kind: capped<M> = N  name: text }` is refused at the template, since `badge<9, 3>` and `badge<5, 4>` would give the selector two types. An alias template (`id => <T> T`, a partial application), a container template (`arr => <T> [T]`), a constructor-application template (`vector`) and an atom template (no form, §12.1) are **not** bases and are refused at a type position: an alias is indirection with no contract for a base to hold, abstractness has nowhere to live on a non-record body, and `vector<float32, 3>` and `vector<float32, 4>` erase to one host type no reader could place a value within. What a base carries is what survives erasure — a value parameter's pin dropped and the field kept, a field whose type mentions a type parameter omitted — so a base's field list may be smaller than its members' and may be **empty**; member dispatch alone has a floor, at least one selector, and a base claiming dispatch with none is refused. The base's facts do not reach a member: a declared member states no `discriminators` — closing pins each selector a parameter pins to its argument, and a `=?` selector is pinned by no argument, so closing does not pin it. A resolved-output consumer recognises the base by its constructor and dispatches on its `discriminators`, and never reads a field that carries a parameter (§1.3). A record template named *bare* at an operand of `&` or `^` is a resolver error: a position may name the base, but an operand is flattened, and a template has fields only once its parameters are bound (§5.8). A record template is no referent for a reference-typed slot of a value body (`request: box` over `box => <T> { v: T }` at `request?: meta.ref<meta.record>` is refused, a template denoting no value until applied, §5.12), and a document names there the application a declaration gives it.

**Templates are not directly instantiable.** An entry with a non-empty parameter list cannot validate data: a template with any parameter is a resolver error as a data annotation, without exception (§7.2), and a family base is reached by a *position* naming it, never by a tag naming it — the tag names a member.

**Open bodies in output.** While parameters remain unbound, an entry's resolved form is one of two values (§8.1).
