**Element order.** Order is a facet a container states (§5.3): `ordered` says whether two values differing only in order are one value — `true` for an array unless it says otherwise, `false` for a set by `meta.set_type`'s pin, and `false` for a map unless it says otherwise. It never changes what a document may write. A set is thus unordered as a *value*: uniqueness and set equality are what the type states, and element order carries no meaning at a set-typed position. **Output keeps the order written either way.** In resolved output a set's elements, like an unordered map's entries, appear in source declaration order — for `meta.enum_type.members`, `numeric.integer_type.members`, `numeric.decimal_type.members`, `unicode.text_type.members` and `net.uri_type.schemes` alike — so that two resolved documents are compared as §8 writes them, with no table of unordered positions to consult and no canonicalisation step; a reader keeps the author's order, which an ordinal-assigning encoding also needs. A refinement that shrinks a member set keeps the surviving members in their inherited order.

These rules apply uniformly to every set-typed position: the kernel's `meta.enum_set`, the exact tiers' `members`, user-defined set-typed fields, and set values produced by the positional fill rule (§5.6). A void element at a set-typed position is rejected — `meta.set_type` pins `voidable: = false` (§5.3) — and a set may be empty unless its type states `min_items: 1`, as `set<T>` does not and every member set of the kernel and the libraries does.


### 7.6 The Void Sentinel Under a Schema

[TSON-DATA] §2.9 defines the void sentinel and its data-value positions. `_` is not itself a type; the type whose sole conforming value is `_` is `void` (§4.2). It is the one spelling of void: the token `null` is a string, or a parse error, or a validation error, by the position's type (§7.3).

This document extends the position rules of [TSON-DATA] §2.9:

| Position                                  | `_` permitted? | Meaning                                                                                          |
|-------------------------------------------|----------------|--------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------|
| Array element (schema in scope)           | conditional    | Permitted only when the array type is `voidable`, written `[T?]` (§5.3)                           |
| Tuple element (schema in scope)           | conditional    | Permitted only when the position is `voidable` (§5.3); the void element occupies its slot        |
| Record field value (schema in scope)      | conditional    | Permitted when the field is **voidable** — its type carries `?` (§5.2) — whatever its name's mark; the field is then present with a void value. At a field that is not voidable, a validation error, whatever the modifier — at a defaulted field the fix is omission, which injects the default (§5.2). At a voidable group member, `_` is present and chooses its option (§5.11). A dependent field and its sibling are never voidable (§5.2) |
| Map key (schema in scope)                 | no             | Resolver error — restates [TSON-DATA] §2.9's resolver-layer rule; keys define membership and are always required |
| Map entry value (schema in scope)         | conditional    | Permitted only when the map type is `voidable`, written `{K => V?}` (§5.3); the entry is then present with a void value ([TSON-DATA] §2.9) and counts toward the size bounds. A namespace value's entries are never void (§4.1) |
| Field-type position (type-definition grammar) | no         | Parse error — use type parameters (§5.10)                                                        |
| Type expression position (type-definition grammar) | no    | Parse error                                                                                      |
| Type-def body (declaration right-hand side) | no             | Parse error — use `{}` for an empty record                                                       |
| Field modifier value (`~`/`=`)            | no             | Resolver error — `~ _` and `= _` are refused; "may be missing or written `_`, and nothing else" is the type slot's to say, `a?: void?` (§5.2) |

When a schema is in scope, a void element requires the governing container type to permit it. Void elements occupy positional slots, and size constraints count all slots. A tuple's voidable position requires an explicit `_` — the tuple's length is fixed by its type, and short tuples are validation errors (§5.3).


### 7.7 Resolver Behaviours at Typed Positions

**Field names in the name type's form.** A document's field name is matched against the record's declared names in the record's name type's `normalization` form (§5.2, §5.5): under a case-folding name type `NAME` is the field `name`, two spellings of one name in one record are a duplicate field, and a name that is no value of the name type can match no declared field and is unrecognised (§7.2). The schema's own references to its fields match the declared spelling exactly; only a document's names are matched in the type's form.

**Typed key equality.** [TSON-DATA] §2.6 defines layered duplicate-key identity: textual identity as the parser's minimum, decoded-value identity for any processor that decodes, and declared-type identity — which can only make *more* keys equal — on top. A schema-aware processor decodes keys by the declared key type's contract (§7.4), so two keys equal under that contract are one key, and a duplicate that only the declared key type relates (`1` and `1.0` under a `number`-keyed map, two spellings of one octet string under a `bytes`-keyed one, `Content-Type` and `content-type` under a text key type whose `normalization` folds case — the textual and decoded-value duplicates of [TSON-DATA] §2.6 are already resolver errors below this layer) is a **Class 2 validation error** at the repeated occurrence, consistent with the set rule (§7.5): identity under a declared *atom* key type is over the key type's value space (§5.5), and under a compound key type — a record, array, map or choice — it is the processor's host equality over what it built, at least the textual identity of [TSON-DATA] §2.6 in a tree the processor builds itself, on §7.5's terms. Which host representation the decoded keys take afterwards is the implementation's concern; the identity contract for an atom key is not. A key typed by an identifier family is a name as well as a key, and the keys of one such map are a look-alike scope (§11.4). A namespace value's keys are names of the family its type's `key_type` states, judged at link (§4.1).

**Empty braces.** [TSON-DATA] §2.8 defers empty-brace disambiguation to declared type information. Under a schema, the expected type at the position supplies it: the resolver transforms an empty-brace value into an empty record or an empty map per the expected type, defaulting to an empty record when the position is untyped. The rule covers exactly the two containers that share the brace form and cannot be told apart when empty; an empty map then faces the map's size bounds like any other. At a position whose expected type is anything else — an array, a tuple, an atom, a choice with no brace-class variant — an empty brace is a **validation error**, reported as a value of the wrong form: an array or tuple has its own empty spelling (`[]`), and admitting `{}` there would let a brace-class value conform to a bracket-class type (§5.4).


### 7.8 Declared Positions: the Open Sum

Where `meta.choice` is the *closed* sum, enumerating its variants, the kernel's `meta.declared_type` is the *open* sum: a value at a position typed by its one instance, `meta.declared`, names its own type by its tag, among the types of the governing closure — the schema's own declarations and its imports (§2.2.3) — and is validated in full against it.

```
declared_type => meta.sum & {}
declared      => !meta.declared_type {}
```

**The data rule.** At a position whose type resolves, past its alias chain, to `meta.declared`:

- a value carrying a tag names its type: the name resolves in the governing namespace exactly as at any other position (§7.2), a tag naming no type of the closure is a resolver error, and the value validates in full against the type it names;
- a value carrying no tag is a **validation error** — *a value at a declared position must name its type* — in every mode, since the position promises no type for an untagged value to be read as (§7.1).

`meta.declared` is SUM-kinded and has no discrimination class (§5.4): it is never a variant of a disjoint choice, and a value at a declared position inside a choice carries the choice's tag and its own. It is what the schema map's entries are: `meta.schema`'s `value_type` is `meta.declared`, so every entry of a schema map names its type by its tag — a constructor for a type, an ordinary type for a value — which is how a resolved form reads (§8.1), and how a data document of `meta.schema` is read at all.

A position admits a value of a foreign schema by importing it: a data document's namespace is the union of its imports' closures (§3.3.4), and no value opens a schema of its own — a directive at a value position is a parse error ([TSON-DATA] §3.3). A schema that means to admit a type from a document it does not import has no way to say so, by design: a foreign schema that kept its own names would put two namespaces in one document, so either a tag's meaning would depend on its position or the data would carry a prefix, and neither is taken.


## 8. Resolver Output

The resolver's output for a schema is a value of the kernel's `meta.schema` — a map from each name to the value its entry resolves to (§4.1): the output records and their conventions (§8.1), template instantiation and identity (§8.2), and aliases (§8.3). Each construct's mapping to its value is defined with the construct in §4–§5. Resolved output and source are one grammar: output is source with sugar expanded, bodies flattened and chains computed, and an entry written as a constructor application resolves to itself.


### 8.1 Output Records

**An entry is its value.** A schema map's entry resolves to the value its declaration denotes, with no wrapper around it: `person => !meta.record { fields: { … } }`, `integer => !numeric.integer_type {}`, `age => !numeric.integer_type { chain: { supertypes: [integer] }  min: 0 }`, `meta.type_name => !meta.alias { target: meta.identifier }`, `vec => !meta.template { … }`, a value entry `orders => !api { … }`, and a nested map `geo => !meta.schema { … }`. Values are annotated with the structurally appropriate constructor: `!meta.record` for vocabulary bodies (constructors, §4.2) and for every closed record; `!meta.record_template` for a record template; a binding record headed by the applied constructor for every closed non-alias definition (§5.6); `!meta.alias { target: T }` for alias-kind entries; `!meta.template { … }` for a held open entry (§5.10); an ordinary type's tag for a value entry (§4.1); and `!meta.schema` for a nested map. `meta.schema`'s `value_type` is `meta.declared`, and every entry names its type by its tag (§7.8); a constructor's path is written as the document that declared it declares it (`!meta.record`, `!numeric.decimal_type`, `!net.uuid_type`, `!enum`), and a name at every slot as the described schema writes it.

**A resolved form's header is its source's**, and its root is `!schema`: it carries the source's `!!id` (under a fixture identity where one is published) and the source's `!!import`s, so that the namespace in which its names resolve is the one the source resolved in; nothing in the document marks it resolved, and a processor reads it as a resolved form because its caller says so (§10.1). The kernel's own resolved form carries its `!!id` alone and is read under the pre-loaded kernel. Nothing is supplied beside the document.

Four things a reader might expect are deliberately not on the entry. There is no `constructor` field: whether an entry is a constructor is whether it is `meta.top` or IS-A it, derivable from its chain (§4.2). There is no `kind` field: an entry's kind is derived (below). There is no `source` field: a construction's constructor is the value's tag, a refinement's origin is the chain's nearest member, and the one fact a wrapper once held that nothing else states — the application an entry is the instantiation of — rides on the entry's key as `@meta.application` (§8.2). And there is no `members` on a reference and no `disjoint` on the entry: the one derived index a body carries lives in the body it is derived over, and a reference's admitted set is derived and never written (§4.2).

**A type carries its chain.** Every type body — every value whose constructor IS-A `meta.type` — carries `meta.type`'s one field, `chain`, a `meta.type_chain` (§4.1): `supertypes`, the closed IS-A chain, every element a path without arguments, complete to a fixed point through applied parents (§5.8) and sorted; `subtypes`, the inverse over the entries of this map; and, on a record template alone, `parents`, the parents as written, one of which may be an application (§5.10). An empty chain is omitted. A composition's fields are flattened and its `chain` stands among them first. `meta.alias`, `meta.template` and a value entry carry no chain — a hop, an open body and a value carry no IS-A, which is the rule. A declaration never writes `chain` and a constructor never declares a field of that name (§4.1).

**Open entries.** An open entry takes one of two forms (§5.10). A **record template** is `!meta.record_template { fields: { … }  parameters: [ … ] }`: an ordinary record body read as the vocabulary, its fields `meta.record_template_field`s — `( type | type_param )  optional?  voidable?  role?  ( value | value_param )?` — a parameter standing as a field's `type_param` or `value_param` or as an argument of an application in a field's `type` or in a parent, its `discriminators` stated as the family's, its `extension` each application's, and `chain.parents` the parents as written. Every other open entry is an instance of the kernel's `meta.template` constructor — `template => meta.top & { parameters: [meta.template_param]  template: text }`, so `set => !meta.template { parameters: [{ name: T  type: meta.type_ref }]  template: "!meta.set_type { element_type: T }" }`. The parameters are a list of `meta.template_param` records, in declaration order — the arity, order and types an application binds against: `template_param => { name: meta.param_name  type: meta.type_ref  bound?: meta.type_ref }`, where `type` is the type an argument is read as, derived from the body and narrowed by a written type, and `bound` is present only where `type` is `meta.type_ref` (§5.10). It is a list of records rather than a map, which would lose the order arguments are positional in, or two parallel lists, which would be free to drift. `type` is a derived fact that may also carry what an author wrote — `<N: int8>` lives nowhere else — so a resolved-form read verifies it rather than recomputing it (below). A `meta.template`'s application is **text**, the held body as written under §5.10's one-spelling rule, an application in it written as the reference it is (`!meta.alias { target: pair<uuid B> }`); what is compared — for identity, on reading a resolved form, and between two resolvers' outputs — is the **parsed form** of the text, never the text, so whitespace is free (§5.10). A `!meta.template` carries no chain, `extension` or `discriminators`: it is not a type, and an application of it is its expansion (§8.2). A consumer of closed entries never meets either (§1.3), and dispatches on a record template's stated facts without reading a field that carries a parameter; a consumer that reads a `!meta.template`'s held text needs a schema parser, which a resolved-form read already requires (below). Both constructors are resolver vocabulary: nothing is ever typed by them, and a source declaration applying either directly is a resolver error — the parameter list `<…>` is the authored spelling of an open entry (§5.10).

**Kind is derived.** A consumer that needs an entry's kind (§4.1) derives it from the entry's value, in this order, and every conforming resolver derives the same answer: **(1)** the value is a `!meta.template` → TEMPLATE, an open entry with no kind of its own; **(2)** else the value's constructor head is `meta.alias` → ALIAS — the head lookup would say PRODUCT, since the kernel's `meta.alias` is itself a product, and §4.1 makes ALIAS a kind the alias form confers rather than a base kind; **(3)** else the entry is `meta.top` or IS-A it — it is a constructor — → the base-kind name among its own `chain.supertypes` (`meta.atom`, `meta.sum`), each counted only as the kernel's own entry, or PRODUCT if none: a constructor's kind states what its *instances* are, not what its own body is (`numeric.integer_type` has a `!meta.record` body and is ATOM-kinded); **(4)** else the value's constructor head is a constructor → the kind of that constructor, ATOM, PRODUCT or SUM, a `!meta.record_template` being PRODUCT; **(5)** else the head is an ordinary type → the entry is a value entry, and its kind is its type's, NAMESPACE where the type is a namespace type. A kind is judged by origin, not spelling: a schema's own record named `product` reaches none of the kernel's entries, so a composition onto it is PRODUCT by structural default.

**The `meta.alias`, `meta.record_field`, `meta.record_template_field` and `meta.field_map` records.** An alias body is `!meta.alias { target: T }`, where `target` is a reference — a bare name for every closed alias, and an application with its arguments where the alias is still open and so inside a held body (`!meta.template { … template: "!meta.alias { target: pair<uuid B> }" }`, §5.10), so that a partial application states the arguments it binds. An alias is a hop: `target` names the immediate referent, and a use site elsewhere in the output that names the alias names the alias (§8.3). A record's `fields` is a **`meta.field_map`** — an ordered `meta.map` from each field's name, as text, to its `meta.record_field` (§4.1, §5.2): the name is the key, written once by its spelling, and each `meta.record_field` carries its `type` (a `meta.field_type_ref` value — a type's name, or a sibling reference `kind!` written as written) and the four facts a reader consults — `optional` (the name's `?`, default `false`), `voidable` (the type's `?`, default `false`), `role` (`FREE`, `DEFAULT` or `FIXED`, default `FREE`) and `value`, typed `type!` and so read against the field's own `type` — each default omitted in output, and `value` present exactly when `role` is not `FREE`. Three invariants hold over them: `value` is present exactly when `role` is not `FREE`; `DEFAULT` requires `optional: true`, a default on a key that is always written being a value nothing can reach; and `FIXED` requires `voidable: false`, since the `_` decision is taken before the pin is consulted. A record field is closed: its `type` names a type or a sibling, and its `value` is a literal, never a parameter. A record template's field is a `meta.record_template_field`, which labels a parameter standing for the whole type (`type_param`) or the whole value (`value_param`); its `fields` is a `meta.record_template_field_map`. There is no stored omission answer: a reader derives it — a field that is not optional is missing, an optional one with a value injects it, except at a field group's member, which the group governs and never supplies (§5.2, §5.11). A `meta.record` body also carries `extension` (omitted at `OPEN`), `discriminators` (present where the record is a member-dispatched base), `name_type` (present exactly where it was stated, §5.2) and `groups`; a `meta.record_template` carries the same and `parameters`. The `meta.field_map`'s keys are text judged by the record's name type — `meta.field_name` where neither the record nor a supertype states one — and two keys that are one value under it are a resolver error (§5.2).

**`optional` and `voidable` in output.** The series' two kinds of nothing have one name each wherever a record states them (§5.2). `optional` — the slot may be missing — is a field of `meta.record_field` and of `meta.field_group`, where it is the group's own `?`, beside `optional_members` for the members that may be missing once their option is chosen (§5.11). `voidable` — the slot's value may be void — is a field of `meta.record_field`, `meta.tuple_element`, `meta.array` and `meta.map`, where it speaks of the field's value, the position, the element and the entry's value. A container's part is never missing, so a container has no `optional`; a map's key is never void, so `meta.map.voidable` is its value's alone; both are structural, and no rule has to refuse them. Each is a boolean defaulting to `false` and omitted there. There is no `element_state` and no `state` field: a binding record carrying one is refused as a member its vocabulary does not declare (§5.5), never read as the other fact.

**Positional form and references.** A slot typed by a reference holds a bare name — `type: text`, `element_type: meta.record_field`, `target: meta.identifier` — or, where an application is open, the reference with its arguments, written unquoted ([TSON-DATA] §7.4), and a sibling reference where `allow_sibling` admits it. Canonical output writes an application with no whitespace but one space between arguments and no comma; identity is the parsed form (§8.2). `meta.record_field.type` is an atom, so a `meta.record_field` has a positional form and a document may write `fields: { x => text }` for `fields: { x => { type: text } }`. Canonical output writes the explicit record, tagged: `fields: { x => !meta.record_field { type: text } }`. The untagged record and the positional form are read as the same value, `meta.field_map`'s `value_type` typing the position.

**Reading parameter references.** **A parameter reference appears only inside an open entry's own body** — a held text, or a record template's fields and parents — and every reference outside one names an entry and is read directly against the schema. Within a held body a parameter stands as its name, read against the entry's `parameters` list first, then the schema's namespace, the same precedence used during source-level parsing (§5.10); within a record template's fields a parameter is labelled, `type_param` or `value_param`, and among an application's arguments in a field's `type` or in a parent it stands as its name, read by the same precedence. A constructor head in held text resolves in the schema's namespace as it did at source level (§3.3.1). A token in a closed body that would resolve only as a parameter is an unresolved reference, the verdict every other reference form gives it.

**`@meta.application` is the one fact the value does not state.** An entry that closes a template application carries, on its schema-map key, `@meta.application:box<int32>` — the application as written, the reference with its argument list, nested arguments unreduced (`@meta.application:pair<box<text> int32>`), each bare-name argument past its alias chain and a value argument a token (§8.2, §8.3). It is the entry's identity as an instantiation, and an entry of any kind can carry it: a record template's declared member, a value entry closed from a templated instance, the alias a template's application closes to, a self-reaching application's instantiation entry (§8.2). It is a fact about the entry and not about a type, so it lives beside the entry as `@meta.synthetic` does, is derived — an author's `@meta.application` is a resolver error, as a written `chain` is — and is **read as stated** when a resolved form is read, not recomputed (§6, §10.1), which is what lets an imported schema's instantiations unify with a later schema's. Nothing else records provenance: which operator a refinement used, and what a subtracted record was subtracted from, are what was written, nothing reads them after resolution, and the source document has them.

**The derived indexes: `subtypes` and `disjoint`.** Two indexes are computed by the resolver and never set by a declaration, each living in the body it is derived over. `chain.subtypes` is the transitive inverse of `supertypes` across the schema's namespace — "what is in family X?" — and `supertypes` answers "is this in family X?" by name; a parent written as an application contributes, once the composing entry closes, the name of the entry its arguments name, and a declared member additionally the head (§5.8), and a record template is indexed by its own name, its members being the applications a declaration names (§5.10). `supertypes`' standing differs from `subtypes`': `subtypes` is a cache, fully derivable and never trusted, while `supertypes` is part of a type's serialized meaning — the chain and a flat body cannot be recomputed from each other, and for the atom family the hop lives only there, since desugaring erases the surface distinction between refining an instance (`age => !integer ^ { … }`, IS-A `integer`) and constructing a fresh instance (`port => !numeric.integer_type { … }`, IS-A nothing), both serializing under the `!numeric.integer_type` tag with an identical body shape. Construction and expansion are not IS-A: a sugar construction (`ids => [order]`) carries no supertypes (§5.6), and a template's expansion carries whatever chain the expansion's own `^` or `&` gives it — a record template's declared member IS-A the template (§5.10) — but a minted record takes no edge to a parent application nothing declares, and is a member of nothing (§8.2). **`disjoint`** is `meta.choice`'s: `choice => meta.sum & { variants: [meta.type_ref]  disjoint?: boolean }`, and for every choice, declared or synthetic, the resolver writes `true` or `false` beside the variants it is derived over — discrimination-class distinctness, a total, two-valued derivation (§5.4). It sits there rather than on the entry because a variant list is the only thing it is a fact *about*, and §7.2's closed-record rule refuses `disjoint` on any other body, so `meta.declared_type` needs no carve-out; the `@meta.disjoint` annotation (§5.4), when present, is the author's assertion checked against this derived value at schema load, and does not appear in the value itself. A reference's admitted set is derived at link and never written (§4.2). Like every name in resolver output, the names in these indexes are tokens in the described schema's own namespace (§2.2.3): an index never reaches outside the resolution that wrote it, and a consumer follows the entries in the defining schema's output.

**Reading a resolved form.** A resolved form is a data document of `meta.schema`; reading one as the resolver's output rather than as data is the caller's explicit choice (§10.1), as the form's header carries no mark. The reader is told the root's type and reads under the form's own imports — the kernel's own form, which imports nothing, under the pre-loaded kernel — and nothing is supplied beside the document. Then: `chain.subtypes`, `disjoint` in every choice body, and the derived annotation `@meta.synthetic` on synthetic entries' keys (§8.2) MUST be discarded and recomputed, so none can be forged into a document to change how it reads; an entry's kind is derived on the same terms and was never in the document to trust (above). `chain.supertypes` and `chain.parents` are **read as stated**, never recomputed, with integrity verified — every listed supertype must exist, the list must be closed to a fixed point, an atom's supertypes must share the entry's constructor tag, and a record's must be consistent with the flattened `fields` — because a flat body and its chain cannot be recomputed from each other (§4.1). `@meta.application` is read as stated (above). Author-written annotations, key- and value-position alike (§6), are preserved as data, a restated field's in the order §5.8 fixes. The closed-entry rule (§5.10) MUST be verified — no token inside a closed body that resolves only as a parameter (§5.6); a `!meta.template`'s held text is parsed and **re-resolved as source** against the form's namespace, since a held body is never read against a vocabulary until it closes, and the parsed form MUST equal the one-spelling form §5.10 requires; the text is never compared as text. Each parameter's recorded `type` and `bound` are **verified, not recomputed**: the reader derives them again and checks that a recorded `type` IS-A the derived one and a recorded `bound` IS-A every inherited one — the rule a written type already met at the declaration (§5.10) — so that a written narrowing survives and a stale or altered one is refused, where discarding and recomputing would silently drop every type an author wrote. Every entry carrying `@meta.application` MUST equal the recomputation by substitution from it, and every synthetic entry MUST carry the body its constructor's vocabulary admits — the self-describing checks. A checked annotation (§6) is re-checked as it was at load: `@meta.disjoint` against the recomputed `disjoint`. Every record's and enum's names are re-judged against its name type, every reference-typed slot's name against its reference (§4.2, §5.2), and a dependent field's held value against the type its sibling names (§5.2). The family checks of §5.2 — every member of a member-dispatched base pinning each selector, pins distinct as values, nothing composed onto a FINAL record, no minted record a member of anything — are re-run over the form's map and the closure that holds it (§3.3.4), the declaration rules of §5.11 are verified over every `meta.field_group`, and the three `meta.record_field` invariants above are verified per field. A nested map's members are keyed under their paths, and a value entry's payload is read against its type as data. Canonical-form violations (a positional `meta.record_field`, a chain written where none is admitted, a `members` list on a reference) are resolver errors. Within-family retargeting — a document claiming an entry refines a different instance of the same constructor — is internally consistent and undetectable from the document alone; this residual gap is one reason resolved forms are never schema sources (§10.1) and reading one is an explicit act.

**Body patterns** (informative — the construct sections govern):

| Source form | Resolved value | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| Root record `abstract {}` | PRODUCT; `!meta.record { chain: { subtypes: [...] }  fields: {}  extension: ABSTRACT }`; no supertypes | `meta.top` |
| `meta.type`, a base kind | PRODUCT; `!meta.record { chain: { supertypes: [meta.top …] … }  …  extension: ABSTRACT }` | `meta.type`, `meta.atom`, `meta.sum` |
| Fresh record `{ fields }` | PRODUCT; `!meta.record { fields: { … } }`; no supertypes | `person`, `meta.record_field` |
| Composition `A & B & { ... }` | `!meta.record { chain: { supertypes: [A B …transitive] }  fields: { …flattened } }`; kind per §5.5 | `employee` |
| Refinement `T ^ { ... }` | `chain.supertypes: [T …]`; materialised body | `production` |
| Subtraction `T - { f }` | `!meta.record { fields: { … } }`; no supertypes (§5.9) | `account_public` |
| Atom constructor `meta.atom & { ... }` | ATOM; IS-A `meta.top`; `chain.supertypes: [meta.atom meta.top meta.type]` | `numeric.integer_type`, `meta.enum_type`, `meta.reference_type` |
| Product constructor `meta.product & { ... }` | PRODUCT; IS-A `meta.top`; `chain.supertypes: [meta.product meta.top meta.type]` | `meta.record`, `meta.array`, `meta.record_template` |
| Sum constructor `meta.sum & { ... }` | SUM; IS-A `meta.top`; `chain.supertypes: [meta.sum meta.top meta.type]` | `meta.choice`, `meta.declared_type` |
| Constructor refinement `T ^ { ... }` | IS-A `meta.top` through `T`; `chain.supertypes: [T …]` | `meta.set_type`, `meta.namespace`, `enum` |
| Instance template `<T, N> !C { ... }` | `!meta.template { parameters: [{ name: T  type: meta.type_ref } { name: N  type: … }]  template: "!C { ... }" }` (§5.10) | `vector`, `set`, `meta.ref`, `meta.tuple1` |
| Constructor instance `!T {}` | kind from `T`'s family; `!T {}`; no supertypes | `integer`, `meta.declared`, `uuid` |
| Value refinement `!I ^ { v }` | `!ctor { chain: { supertypes: [I …] }  v }` | `age`, `numeric.count`, `meta.type_ref` |
| Namespace type `!meta.namespace { key_type: K  value_type: V }` | PRODUCT; `!meta.namespace { … }`; no supertypes | `meta.schema`, `api` |
| Sugar or constructor-application body | construction in place; binding-record value; no supertypes (§5.6) | `lookup => {text => integer}`, `meta.field_map` |
| Record template `<T> { ... }` | `!meta.record_template { fields: { … }  parameters: [{ name: T  type: meta.type_ref }] }`, a family base nameable at a type position, ABSTRACT by construction, `chain.parents` as written (§5.10) | `container`, `pair` |
| Sugar form at a use site | lifts to a synthetic entry — closed (constructor body) or open (`!meta.template`) per §5.3; the use site holds a bare reference | `[text]`, `{text => integer}`, `[T; N]` in a template body |
| Template application | the declaration naming it, with `@meta.application` on its key; else its expansion, a closed synthetic, beside which a self-reaching application keeps an instantiation entry, a `!meta.alias` to the synthetic carrying `@meta.application` (§8.2) | `string_triple => vector<text, 3>`, a use site's `set<text>` |
| Value entry `!T { … }`, `T` an ordinary type | the value as read against `T`, `T`'s tag on it; no chain (§4.1) | `orders => !api { … }`, `create_order => !operation { … }` |
| Nested map `!schema { … }` | `!meta.schema { … }`, its members resolved and keyed by name, each an entry of the document by path (§4.1) | `geo => !schema { … }` |
| Choice `(A \| B)` | SUM; `!meta.choice { variants: [...]  disjoint: … }`, the fact always recorded in the body (§5.4) | `contact_method` |
| Field group `( a: T \| b: U )` | members flattened into `fields` as `optional: true`; grouping in `groups`, one list of members per option (§5.11) | `numeric.integer_type`, `meta.record_template_field` |
| Abstract base with selectors `abstract { a: T =?  … }` | PRODUCT; `!meta.record { … extension: ABSTRACT  discriminators: [a] }`; members in `subtypes` pin `a` (§5.2) | `pet` |
| Alias `name` | ALIAS; `!meta.alias { target: name }`; no chain — a bare name, never a fully-bound application, which is an instantiation in place | `id => uuid` (§8.3) |

The `meta.schema` type is a map from type names to values; schema lookup is by path. Declared entries are keyed by their declared names, nested maps nested; synthetic entries and self-reaching instantiation entries are keyed by their internal names at the root, each synthetic entry's key carrying the derived `@meta.synthetic` marker (§6, §8.2).


### 8.2 Instantiation and Synthetic Entries

Resolver output contains internally named entries beyond the declared ones, and one identity discipline covers them all. **Every application materialises, as its expansion**: a sugar form or constructor application at a use site lifts to a **synthetic entry** — closed or open per §5.3's lift rule — and a fully-bound application of a template materialises its **expansion**, the closed synthetic entry the substituted form lands on, keyed on its content (§5.10). Nothing is carried structurally in place: a use site holds a bare reference to its entry, and an argument-bearing reference in output means an open application inside a template body or a record template's fields and parents, an open alias's target, or an `@meta.application`, nothing else (§8.1). A declaration's own body resolves in place as a construction (§5.6) and is the one thing that never lifts.

*Trigger positions.* Lifting and materialisation fire wherever an application is **named at a type position**: record field types, group member types, tuple element types, array and map element positions, choice variants, arguments at any depth, and a **refinement source** — `x => box<text> ^ { n: integer }` materialises `box<text>`, or unifies it with the declaration that owns it in either declaration order, and `x` IS-A the entry it denotes, as §5.7 promises. A top-level type-def body that is a fully-bound template application makes the declaration the entry itself (below). Composition operands remain restricted to named type references, optionally with arguments; inline structural forms there are resolver errors (§5.8, §12.1), and an application at a composition operand is subsumed as an operand and mints nothing, contributing its fields and its head's ancestry and owing no edge to itself (§5.8). An *open* application inside a template body does not materialise — it closes when its template does (§5.10).

*An application nothing declares is its expansion.* A use site naming `set<text>` names the synthetic `!meta.set_type { element_type: text }` lands on — the entry a `[text]`-style sugar or another template expanding to the same form also lands on; a use site naming `box<int32>` names a closed synthetic record, keyed on its content, which `crate<int32>` over a template with the same field list lands on too. No instantiation entry is minted and nothing records the application: the expansion records nothing a value can tell apart, since it admits exactly the synthetic's values. The one exception is a **self-reaching expansion**: `nested => <T> ( T | [nested<T>] )` closed at `nested<text>` names itself inside its own body, so its content cannot be written without a name, and the knot is tied on the application, which keeps an **instantiation entry** — a `!meta.alias` to the synthetic, keyed on the canonical application, carrying `@meta.application` (§5.10.1). The rule is total: whether closing reached the application again decides it. **A minted record is a member of nothing.** A minted record whose chain would name a declared type — `k: dog_of<text>` over `dog_of => <T> pet & { … }`, or a parent application a declaration owns — would be a family member with no name a read, a tag or a binding could use, and is a resolver error at the declaration that wrote the application (§5.2). A parent application nothing declares gives a minted record no chain edge: `u: labelled<text>` over `labelled => <T> box<T> & { label: text }`, beside `w: box<text>`, loads, the minted record IS-A nothing. A minted record gains no IS-A edge later; a declaration's operand still reaches it — `vip => customer & box<int32>`, beside a use site's `box<int32>`, is IS-A that synthetic and stands where `crate<int32>` is expected, the two applications being one type. **A declaration names only itself.** An unnamed form with a declaration's content is not that declaration: beside `text_list => [text]`, a field written `t: [text]` names the synthetic, and `!text_list [a]` there is refused by §7.2; collecting would need a new rule for every form kind and would make two declarations sharing a body ask whether they interchange, and they are two types. **A diagnostic names what the author wrote**, and nothing for a form with no spelling: a minted entry is named by its application as written (`'b<10>': '5' is less than the minimum 10`), a reference's refusal lists `meta.ref<net.uuid_type>` where it would have listed an entry minted for it, and a minted record is named as the body a record is written with (`unknown field 'nope' on '{ v: int32 }'`).

*Two identities, and one sentence that joins them to §10.2.* **Bytes identify a document, for distribution and pinning; names and parsed structure identify an entry, for typing.** Neither stands in for the other: a whitespace edit changes a document's digest (§10.2) and none of its entries, and two spellings of one form in a schema are one entry. **A declared entry's identity is its declaring document and its name**, its path from that document's root (§2.2.3, §4.1): two declarations are two types however alike their bodies — `stock_id => !net.uuid_type {}` and `other_id => !net.uuid_type {}` are two atoms, and a purely structural rule would collapse them and destroy a distinction §7.2 enforces (§5.5). **A declaration whose body denotes a type is that type's entry, in both lift channels**: `text_list => [text]` is the construction in place (§5.6), and `bx => box<text>` is the instantiation in place — the substituted binding record its value, closed under the author's name, `@meta.application` on its key, with nothing minted beside it and no alias hop. A use-site application resolves to a declaration that owns it where one exists, so "two fully-bound applications denote the same entry" holds within a schema. Two declarations naming one application are **two entries**, neither privileged and neither collapsed — what `a => { v: text }` beside `b => { v: text }` already is, and so what `t => <T> { v: T }` with `a => t<text>` and `b => t<text>` has to be; where the pair is a discriminated family §5.2's pin-distinctness rule refuses it, exactly as it refuses the hand-written pair. What this gives up is stated plainly: a content-derived name is a function of the form alone, which is what lets two independently resolved namespaces agree on it across the import merge (below); a declared name cannot carry that, so a schema writing `box<text>` that has never seen the schema declaring `bx` lands on the content-keyed expansion while that schema calls it `bx`, and the merge sees two names for one form. That costs nothing a value can tell — both entries admit exactly the same values, and a use site in the importer resolves to the declaration owning the form — and it cannot reach a family, a family member being declared. **A minted entry's identity is its canonical content**, since it has no declared name to be its identity; "identity is structural" is true of the minted half only, one rule per family:

- **Self-reaching instantiation entries** — two fully-bound applications denote the same entry if and only if their **canonical applications are structurally equal**: the application with every argument's alias chain followed to its terminal entry and every nested application resolved to its entry's name (§8.3), compared member-by-member — heads, argument order, a reference argument against a reference argument, a token against a token under the rule below. Following the chain is what makes a *rename* transparent: `box<user_id>` over `user_id => uuid` denotes the same type as `box<uuid>`, because an alias is the same type under another name (§5.7's table) — where `box<!uuid ^ {}>` and `box<!net.uuid_type {}>`, a refinement and a fresh instance, keep entries of their own. The canonical application is exactly what `@meta.application` records, so identity is a single-level structural comparison of it, and spelling variance never forks an entry; the name the author wrote is provenance, not identity, and survives at the use site that wrote it. A declared instantiation's identity is its name (above); a use site reaches it by the canonical application, compared on these terms.
- **Identity is the parsed form.** A reference is compared as its head and its arguments, each a reference or a token — never as text, and never by the channel a processor filed an argument in: `box< text, >` at an element type and `box<text>` at a type position close to one entry, and `names<red>` is one application whether `red` was read as a reference or filed as a value at its parameter.
- **Value arguments compare as values, and are recorded as written.** A literal argument is recorded in `@meta.application` (and substituted into the body) as the token the author wrote, so resolver output round-trips the spelling; identity, however, compares the value the token denotes under [TSON-DATA] §4 — exactly the equivalence §4.3 states and no wider. Radix, digit separators, and a redundant sign fall away (`255`, `0xFF`, `0b1111_1111`, `0o377`, and `+255` are one argument), a float's written scale falls away (`.5`/`0.5`, `1.0`/`1.00`/`1e0`), and `.inf`/`.infinity` are one value; quoted strings compare by text. What does **not** fall away is the base type: §4 resolves `1` to an integer and `1.0` to a float, so those remain two arguments even though one magnitude covers both. `vector<float32, 255>` and `vector<float32, 0xFF>` therefore denote one entry, and a choice listing `[float32; 255]` and `[float32; 0xFF]` as two variants is the same-variant-twice error of §5.4, not an accepted non-disjoint choice.
- **Closed synthetic entries** — structural equality of the resolved binding record: one entry per distinct concrete form schema-wide, whichever spelling or template produced it, and wherever in the document it was written — inside a nested map included, synthetics minting at the root (§4.1). A closed synthetic carries the **constructor** it builds as its tag, never the application that produced it, which is what lets every channel land on it.
- **Open synthetic entries** — structural equality of the **parsed** held body **up to consistent renaming of parameters** (`<T, N>` and `<A, B>` over the same shape are the same template), normalised by renaming parameters positionally (`p0`, `p1`, …) before comparing; the text a `!meta.template` carries is never compared as text (§5.10). This is what "one spelling" exists for: `<T> { a: [T] }` written twice mints one entry, and an entry's content-derived name is computed over the renamed parsed form.

The channels dedupe against each other's products: `[order; 1..]` written directly in a plain declaration and the same form arising inside a materialised template land on **one** closed synthetic entry, because both comparisons occur after names have meaning, over resolved structure. The moment is normative: desugar-time lifting *creates* a synthetic entry, but its identity settles **after Pass 2**, when references have resolved — eagerly-lifted synthetics that become structurally identical under resolution merge into one entry, so the one-entry-per-form rule holds schema-wide regardless of which moment produced each candidate. **The merge pass is required**, not an optimisation: a closed lift hashes its binding record at desugar, before its inner applications are rewritten to entry names, while an open lift hashes the closed record at materialisation, so without a pass that re-derives every synthetic's identity from its resolved record, `[box<text>]` written directly and `[box<T>]` closed with `T := text` land on two entries for one type (§5.10). The first occurrence materialises; later occurrences reuse; recursive references resolve to the entry's own name (§5.10.1). Closure consults templates and never modifies them: closing `grid<pixel, 4>` after `grid<pixel, 3>` reuses the template entry and both open synthetics untouched while producing fresh closed entries. A refinement source's application closes on the same terms in either declaration order: whether `tb => box<text>` is declared before or after `x => box<text> ^ { … }`, `x` IS-A one entry for `box<text>`, the declaration's where there is one.

*Synthetic entries are an implementation's own.* Their names are resolver-chosen and their shape is unconstrained beyond producing the required resolved output: a processor is free to mint one per schema, share them, alpha-normalise their parameters, or keep them however its own resolver requires, and the lift targets are never kernel-declared as such (§5.3). An author never writes one and never references one; the one thing an author does is name an application they want to reuse, and the spelling for that is an alias — `order_page => page<order>` — which is one line, gives the application an identity and a diagnostic located at the author's own declaration, and is RECOMMENDED over repeating the application (§8.1).

*Entry names are internal.* Minted entries are named by the resolver, and the names are **not part of this specification's conformance surface**: an implementation chooses them freely, subject to two rules and one recommendation. Freshness (MUST): an internal name is a valid identifier and collides with no declared entry and no other internal entry. Stability within a resolution (MUST): all references to one entry use one name. Determinism (SHOULD): names SHOULD be derived from the entry's identity-bearing content — the canonical application for a self-reaching instantiation, the resolved body structure for a closed synthetic, the parameter-normalised body for an open one — a readable head plus a structural hash, for instance, a dotted name contributing its text with each dot written `_` and a path under the kernel's `meta` its member's name alone. Content-derived names keep re-resolution output diff-stable, and they are what makes independently-resolved namespaces agree on their internal names wherever their structures agree — which is what lets the import merge (below) unify rather than collide. Internal names do not exist at source level (there is nothing to author), MUST NOT be relied on across resolutions or implementations, and resolved-form comparison between implementations is structural — equality up to renaming of internal entries, with set-typed fields compared as written, in source declaration order (§7.5). Two consequences for a **consumer** follow and are stated because a wrong pick is invisible until someone writes a configuration: **no consumer contract may require a derived name** — a binding map, a generated class table, a configuration file keys on a name some declaration gave the application, and a processor holding both SHOULD show and accept the declared name wherever it shows or accepts one, falling back to the entry's own only for an entry no declaration names; and **the declared applications of one template are recoverable from `@meta.application`** — a declared instantiation records the application on its key, so the family a host language spells `Result<T>` is a walk over entries and needs no index. A derived name written in data (`!box_text_04117bb4`) is a valid identifier in the namespace and is not refused; such a document was written by reading one processor's output and fails at the next, whose spelling differs.

*Entry shape.* A declared instantiation: `@meta.application` on its key, the canonical fully-bound application as written; its value the substituted binding record, headed by the applied constructor (§5.6) — closed, with the expansion's kind by derivation and whatever chain the expansion's own `^` or `&` gives it: for a record template's member, `chain.supertypes` holding the template and everything it IS-A, plus the closed parent an open `parents` application named and that parent's head (§5.8), its `extension` the record's own (OPEN unless the template wrote `abstract`), never the base's ABSTRACT, and no `discriminators` — closing pins each selector a parameter pins to its argument, and a `=?` selector is pinned by no argument, so closing does not pin it (§5.10). A synthetic entry resolves under the top-level-construction rule: a binding-record value headed by the constructor (a `!meta.template` while open), no supertypes, `@meta.synthetic` at its key (§6, §8.1). Materialisation also runs the value-level checks that the held body deferred: **every family coherence rule that §5.3, §5.5 and §7.4 state for a literally written body applies again at materialisation, over the operands that were parameters** — `min_items ≤ max_items` for the containers, `min ≤ max` and `min_length ≤ max_length` for the atom families, `min_prefix ≤ max_prefix` and the prefix range for the network families, and any rule a family states that is not a bound pair — together with the conformance of every substituted value to the slot it fills (§5.2, §5.10). A resolver needs no list: the rule an application closes onto is the rule the family already owns for a literal body, asked once more of the closed record. A violation is a resolver error located per §5.10's rule — at the declaration whose text wrote the offending name, with the materialising application as context. For the §5.10 template `vector => <T, N> !meta.array { element_type: T  min_items: N  max_items: N }`, a declaration `hd_row => vector<pixel, 1920>` resolves to

```
@meta.application:vector<pixel 1920>
hd_row => !meta.array { element_type: pixel  min_items: 1920  max_items: 1920 }
```

while `vector<pixel, 1920>` written at a use site lands on `hd_row`, the declaration that owns it. Where no declaration names it, the use-site application and the structurally identical form written directly as `[pixel; 1920]` anywhere in the schema both land on one *closed synthetic* entry — the same body, `@meta.synthetic` at its key, no `@meta.application` — which is what §5.3's "a use-site sugar form mints where a declared application does not" comes to: the sugar and the application are two spellings of one expansion. `[pixel; 1920]` lands on that synthetic beside `hd_row` too, a declaration naming only itself (above).

*Non-exposure and the import merge.* Minted entries are resolver-materialised, not declared: they cannot be named from any schema source, and authors reach a type by writing the application (reusing the entry) or by declaring a name for it, which is then the entry (above). Under the transitive import merge (§2.2.3) the internal entries travel with their namespace — an imported entry's body references them, so they are part of what the import delivers — and they merge by the same structural identities as within a schema: a synthetic's identity is its form and not its declaring document, so two documents that mint one form without importing each other declare one entry, which a closure holding both holds once, the first import's, as it holds one schema reached by two routes; an importing schema whose own application derives an identical entry denotes the merged entry, never a duplicate; and internal names never collide with declared names by construction (§3.4.1). A document whose own sugar form derives a name an import already declares references the import's entry, which must be that form: a declared entry of the name is the collision, and a synthetic of another form is an internal error, the naming function broken. A later schema's application of a template an imported library declared and applied is unified with the library's declared instantiation through the `@meta.application` the library's output carries (§8.1).

*Diagnostics and cross-schema identity.* Internal names MUST NOT be the primary form shown to users: diagnostics surface the source application or form — recoverable from the originating source position, and from `@meta.application` where an entry carries one — located at the declaration whose text wrote the offending name (§5.10). Cross-schema identity of *declared* types is through named declarations or not at all; internal entries carry structural identity only.


### 8.3 Aliases

A type-def body that is purely a type *reference* — a bare name or a template application, with no body record, no `&`, and no construction — produces an ALIAS-kind entry whose value is `!meta.alias { target: T }`, where `target` names an entry (§8.1):

- **Simple leaf** (`id => uuid`): `id => !meta.alias { target: uuid }`. The `target` is the immediate referent, not the ultimate type — alias chains (`user_id → id → uuid`, over `id => uuid` and `user_id => id`) appear as distinct entries, each pointing one hop forward. A `target` may be a qualified name, `geo.address`.
- **Fully-bound template application** (`string_triple => vector<text, 3>`) is **not** an alias: the declaration is the instantiation entry itself, closed in place with the application as `@meta.application` on its key (§5.10, §8.2), exactly as a sugar-form body — `string_triple => [text; 3]` — is a construction in place (§5.3, §5.6). A `!meta.alias` body is a bare name or a still-open application, never a closed one — with the one exception of a self-reaching expansion's instantiation entry, which is a `!meta.alias` to its own synthetic (§8.2).
- **Open template application** (`same_tree => <T> tree<T>`, `uuid_pair => <B> pair<uuid, B>` — an application of a named template with at least one parameter still open): the declaration is itself a template, an **alias template** (§5.10); its held body is `!meta.alias { target: pair<uuid B> }`, the application written as the reference it is, so the alias states what it already binds. Alias templates compose during substitution without materialising intermediate entries.
- **An alias of a constructor** (`my_record => meta.record`): `target` names a constructor, and the alias is applied as its target is, in a held body as anywhere (§3.3.1).

A top-level *constructor* application is not an alias — it resolves as a construction in place (§5.6). `!meta.alias { target: X }` is the explicit spelling of the alias `X` and denotes exactly that (§5.5).

The correction is worth its sentence: an earlier revision filed a declared application by its syntax — a parameterised *reference* — and made the declaration a hop to something else's identity, so that `pet.subtypes` read a minted name where the author had written `dogs`, and every surface that showed a name had to follow the hop back. Filed by what it is — a construction — the declared name is the entry, which is what a family's `subtypes` index, a tag, and a diagnostic all key on.

**An alias is a hop, not a rewrite.** Resolved output states an alias chain **as the author wrote it**: a use site — a field type, tuple element, choice variant, argument, or binding-record type slot — that names an ALIAS entry names that entry, and `target` names the next. Nothing is flattened, and no annotation records a source-level name, because the chain itself is the record: an earlier revision rewrote each use site to the chain's terminal entry and attached an annotation naming the first hop, which was a second, lossy representation of a walk that happens anyway — a chain of two hops kept the name of the one that carried nothing — and every pass that needs the terminal entry (reader compilation, discrimination-class classification, inhabitance, variant distinctness, reference membership) walks the chain regardless. A processor MAY collapse a chain **after linking, when it compiles for reading** — an ALIAS entry's reader is its target's, named for the entry doing the referring, which is what gives a diagnostic the alias the author wrote for free — and MUST NOT collapse it in resolved output, which is what two conforming processors compare. The walk stops *at* an argument-bearing target — that is an application, not a hop to another entry, and there is no entry at the end of it until materialisation produces one. A chain with no terminal — `a => b`, `b => a` — is a resolver error (§3.4.1), and §11.5's alias-chain limit bounds a long one.

**Where a chain is followed, and where it is not.** Identity of an application follows its arguments' chains to their terminal entries, because an alias is the same type under another name (§8.2); subsumption at a typed position follows both chains (§7.2); the source of a refinement or composition, a choice variant judged for `void`, a discrimination class, a reference's membership and a qualified name's first segment are all read after following the chain (§4.2, §4.3, §5.4, §5.7, §5.12). What the chain *never* does is carry meaning of its own: nothing that changes how a value reads may live on an alias, and no bundled document declares an annotation that does — a fact that decides reading, such as an alphabet, lives in the type (§5.5), where every position already names it. That is what makes a rename safe to see through.

**Aliases are not supertypes.** ALIAS-kind entries carry no chain and do not contribute to the `supertypes`/`subtypes` graph: `uuid.subtypes` does not list `id`; `id` has no `supertypes`. The three ways to name a type after another, and what each buys, are tabled in §5.7.

**Instantiation entries are not aliases.** A declared instantiation (§8.2) carries its expansion's kind and a binding-record value, not a `!meta.alias` record; an ALIAS entry may *point at* one by name (`alias => string_triple`), and the instantiation itself is a closed, concrete definition. The self-reaching instantiation entry of §8.2, and an alias a template's application closes to (`my => id<text>` over `id => <T> T`), are the `!meta.alias` entries that carry `@meta.application`.


## 9. The Kernel and the Libraries

Six bundled documents carry the series' vocabulary, and one of them is pre-loaded. Implementations MUST pre-load the kernel (§3.4, §10.1) and SHOULD pre-load the libraries. The normative source for each is the companion artifact (§1.5), pinned by content hash at publication; the kernel's pre-loaded in-memory model is authoritative and its document a description of it.

**The placement convention.** A bundled document's root holds the vocabulary an author writes, and its machinery sits under schema maps named for concepts, related concepts kept together — a library's under one map of its own name, the kernel's under six, two of which `numeric.tn` and `net.tn` add to (§2.2.3). The list is curated, entry by entry, so that an author writes `text`, `!enum [A B]`, `set<text>`, `@doc`, `bytes`, `int32`, `date` and `uuid` bare, and `meta.record`, `meta.identifier`, `meta.type_ref`, `meta.ref<meta.record>`, `meta.ref<method>`, `@atom.ordering:TOTAL`, `@meta.disjoint`, `!numeric.decimal_type {}`, `!temporal.date_type {}` and `!net.uuid_type {}` by path:

| Document | Imports | At the root | Under its maps |
|---|---|---|---|
| `meta.tn`, the kernel | nothing (pre-loaded) | `void`, `boolean`, `integer`, `int64`, `text`, `bytes`, `iri`, `iri_reference`, `regex`, `doc`, `enum`, `set`, `schema` | `meta`, the type system: `meta.top`, `meta.type`, the base kinds `meta.atom`/`meta.product`/`meta.sum`, `meta.alias`, `meta.template` and `meta.template_param`, the constructors `meta.record`, `meta.record_template`, `meta.array`, `meta.set_type`, `meta.map`, `meta.namespace`, `meta.tuple`, `meta.choice`, `meta.enum_type` and `meta.declared_type`, the supporting records `meta.type_chain`, `meta.record_field`, `meta.record_template_field`, `meta.field_group` and `meta.tuple_element`, the maps `meta.field_map`, `meta.record_template_field_map` and `meta.schema`, the enums `meta.product_access_type`, `meta.product_size_type`, `meta.field_role` and `meta.record_extension_type`, the set `meta.enum_set`, the references `meta.reference_type`, `meta.ref`, `meta.field_type_ref`, `meta.type_ref` and `meta.identifier_ref`, `meta.identifier` and its aliases `meta.type_name`, `meta.field_name` and `meta.param_name`, `meta.void_type`, `meta.declared`, the annotation machinery `meta.annotation`, `meta.synthetic` and `meta.application`, the checked assertion `meta.disjoint`, and `meta.tuple1` and `meta.voidable_tuple1`. `atom`: the ISO/IEC 11404 datatype properties `atom.ordering`, `atom.bounded`, `atom.exact` and `atom.numeric`, and the mixin `atom.specification`. `numeric`: `numeric.integer_type`, `numeric.integer_size`, `numeric.integer_member_set` and `numeric.count`. `unicode`: `unicode.text_type`, `unicode.text_member_set`, `unicode.normalization`, `unicode.identifier_type`, `unicode.identifier_base` and `unicode.regex_type`. `net`: `net.iri_type`, `net.scheme_set` and `net.scheme_name`. `binary`: `binary.bytes_type` and `binary.bytes_encoding`. |
| `numeric.tn` | the kernel | `number`, `rational`, `complex`, `int8`, `int16`, `int32`, `int128`, `int256`, `uint8`–`uint256`, `float32`, `float64` | `numeric`, beside the kernel's members: `numeric.decimal_type`, `numeric.float_type`, `numeric.rational_type`, `numeric.complex_type`, `numeric.ieee_format`, `numeric.complex_component`, `numeric.decimal_member_set` |
| `temporal.tn` | the kernel | `date`, `time`, `datetime`, `duration`, `period` | `temporal`: `temporal.date_type`, `temporal.time_type`, `temporal.datetime_type`, `temporal.duration_type`, `temporal.period_type` |
| `net.tn` | the kernel | `email`, `ipv4`, `ipv6`, `cidr4`, `cidr6`, `mac`, `hostname`, `host`, `media_type`, `uri_reference`, `uri`, `uuid` | `net`, beside the kernel's members: `net.email_type`, `net.ipv4_type`, `net.ipv6_type`, `net.cidr4_type`, `net.cidr6_type`, `net.mac_type`, `net.hostname_type`, `net.host_type`, `net.media_type_type`, `net.uri_type`, `net.uuid_type` |
| `core.tn` | `numeric.tn`, `temporal.tn`, `net.tn` | the author annotations `deprecated`, `title`, `comment`, `examples`, `read_only`, `write_only` | nothing: it declares no map of its own |
| `policy.tn` | the kernel | nothing | `policy`: `policy.processor` and its parts `policy.identifier_policy`, `policy.script_policy` and `policy.limits`, `policy.restriction_level` and `policy.limit` |

**A library imports the kernel alone; `core.tn` is the bundle.** A schema imports the libraries it uses: one importing `temporal.tn` alone has `date` and the kernel's vocabulary, does not see `int32`, and may declare `int32` as an entry of its own, though not `numeric`, which the kernel declares; one importing `numeric.tn` and `net.tn` has both, their closures meeting at the kernel by identity. Every schema whose closure holds the kernel reserves the kernel's thirteen root names and the map names `meta`, `atom`, `numeric`, `unicode`, `net` and `binary` (§2.2.3). `core.tn` imports the three, so a schema wanting every bundled type imports `core.tn` and reserves those, `temporal` and every root above; `record`, `identifier`, `type_ref`, `field_name`, `bounded`, `value`, `kind` and `core` stay free. No two of the bundled documents declare one root name or one member path, and the maps two of them declare, `numeric` and `net`, are open and the union of their members, so no closure of them collides with itself. `policy.tn` imports the kernel alone; a closure holding it reserves `policy`.

**The kernel, `meta.tn`.** One document, header `!!id` alone and pre-loaded (§3.4): its one wait cycle — the entries around `meta.record`, `meta.array`, `meta.set_type`, `enum`, `meta.map`, `meta.field_map`, `meta.reference_type`, `numeric.integer_type`, `unicode.text_type`, `boolean`, `integer`, `text`, `numeric.count`, `numeric.integer_size`, `meta.field_type_ref`, `meta.type_ref`, `meta.type_chain`, `meta.record_field`, `meta.field_group`, `meta.field_role`, `meta.enum_set` and the arrays their declarations lift — every name its declarations reference, and the concepts that belong with them. The kernel's tag `!schema` names its own root `schema => meta.schema`, an alias, and resolved output writes every nested map `!meta.schema`. A kind and a constructor are the kernel's by declaring document, which is `meta.tn` alone; the libraries' constructors are ordinary constructors of the documents that declare them, and a schema may contribute a member to any kernel map on §2.2.3's terms (`meta => !schema { code_type => unicode.text_type ^ { max_length?: = 4 } }` beside `code => !meta.code_type {}`).

**Every name resolves from the root, inside the bundled documents too.** An entry names a member of a map by its path: `integer => !numeric.integer_type {}`, `record => meta.product & { fields: meta.field_map … }`, `format: numeric.ieee_format`, `@atom.ordering:TOTAL`. There is no lexical scope; the synthetics a document's sugar lifts are its root's.

Each atom family is a constructor/instance pair (§4.2): the constructor lists the family's constraint fields, and the canonical instance lives at the root of the same document — `void`, `integer`, `int64`, `text`, `meta.identifier`, `iri_reference`, `iri`, `regex` and `bytes` in the kernel; `number`, `rational`, `complex`, the sized integers and the floats in `numeric.tn`; `date`, `time`, `datetime`, `duration` and `period` in `temporal.tn`; `email`, `ipv4`, `ipv6`, `cidr4`, `cidr6`, `mac`, `hostname`, `host`, `media_type`, `uri_reference`, `uri` and `uuid` in `net.tn`. Every facet holding a value of the constrained family is typed by that instance (§5.5, §7.4), and every count by `numeric.count` (§9's `numeric` map), the non-negative half of `int64`. Two rules hold across every family and are stated once in `numeric.tn`'s header: `multiple_of`, wherever it appears, is strictly positive, tests the value's magnitude with the sign ignored, tightens only to an integer multiple, and composes with every other facet; and `members`, wherever it appears, has every member satisfying the body's other facets — `unicode.text_type`'s pattern included — and a refinement shrinks the set on the numeric tiers and sets it once on `unicode.text_type` (§5.7, §7.4). Every set-typed field is emitted in source declaration order (§7.5). The classifying annotations the libraries state their atoms with are the kernel's `atom.ordering`, `atom.bounded`, `atom.exact` and `atom.numeric` (§6).

**What the kernel holds, and what the libraries do.** The kernel holds what the type system needs to describe itself — its one wait cycle, every name the cycle's declarations reference (`integer`, `text`, `regex`, `iri`, `boolean`, the references), and the concepts that belong with them (`meta.tuple`, `meta.choice`, `meta.template`, `bytes`, `doc`, `set`) — and the atoms every schema writes. A data-format family most schemas never use belongs in a library outside the kernel, where its names cost only the schemas that import it: the numeric tiers beyond `integer` and `int64`, the temporal families, the network and identifier families. `core.tn` holds the author annotations and nothing else, so that a schema importing a library alone may declare `title` or `deprecated` for itself.

**JSON.** A JSON document is read through the JSON encoding of [TSON-JSON], a second encoding of this model and not a mode of the TSON notation ([TSON-DATA] §6): it maps a JSON number to `number`, JSON `null` to void — admitted where the position is voidable (§7.6) — and a JSON object to a record where its keys are names its record admits, identifiers by default (§5.2). Every type doc in the artifacts describes its value space without reference to any encoding and names TSON text where it means it; the family facts of §5.2 and the `disjoint` fact of §5.4 are the model's, and both encodings read them.

**Guidance for schemas that extend the vocabulary.** Any schema may declare a constructor (§4.2), and three rules keep an extended vocabulary coherent with the kernel's. First, **a slot that names a type is typed by a reference over a constructor, a slot that names a value by a reference over its type, and a slot that holds a sibling reference by `meta.field_type_ref`** (§4.2, §5.2, §5.12): a field whose value is the name of a type a value can have is `meta.type_ref`, or a narrower reference — `request?: meta.ref<meta.record>` admits the names of the record types and refuses `uuid`'s — which is what makes the slot judged at link and visible to every reference mechanism (§8.1, §8.3); a field whose value is a value declared elsewhere, written by its name, is `meta.ref<T>`, which declares the spelling and the judgement at once; a field read against the type a sibling names is `kind!`. The kernel's own `meta.enum_type.name_type`, `meta.record.name_type` and `meta.namespace.key_type` are `meta.identifier_ref`, and `meta.reference_type.of`, `meta.alias.target`, `meta.type_chain`'s lists and `meta.application` are `meta.type_ref`. Second, a schema's own vocabulary — the records its constructors' instances hold, the namespace types it declares — is declared beside its constructors, under a map of its own where it is machinery (`api => !meta.namespace { key_type: method_name  value_type: method }`, §4.1), and reserves what it declares in every importer. Third, the kernel's `regex` atom is pinned to a specification (`atom.specification`, §4.2), and the pin is a strict gate: an implementation MUST implement the pinned dialect as specified — not a host library's near-relative — and MUST document any divergence it cannot avoid; a pattern using a feature outside the pinned dialect is a resolver error at schema load, not a silently host-dependent behaviour. The same discipline applies to every spec-pinned atom an extension declares: the `spec` pin is the contract, and "whatever the platform regex engine does" is never a conforming reading. A constructor composing a base kind directly declares a new *form*, which a processor backs with readers and writers of its own; a schema that wants its own atom family composes a constructor the processor already backs, or declares a form and ships the processor that reads it.


## 10. Schema Resolution Model

Schema URLs are **logical identifiers first**: a URL names a schema, and resolving it does not by itself require a network request. Implementations resolve schema references through a **schema library** — a local store mapping canonical identities ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1) to schema content. Fetching is a permitted but opt-in way to *populate* the library (§10.1, §11.2), not the meaning of a reference.


### 10.1 The Schema Library

When the resolver encounters an `!!import` directive, it looks the reference up in the library — the reference read to its canonical identity in any spelling ([TSON-DATA] §2.2.1); if not found, the resolver does not attempt to fetch unless fetching is enabled (§11.2), and reports one of two outcomes, neither a finding about validity ([TSON-DATA] §8.1). A schema this processor **will not** supply — one it does not hold and will not fetch, a host off its allowlist, an illegal identity, a missing required pin, or one past its size limit (§11.2) — is a **refusal**: a decision of this deployment's configuration, as deployment-specific as an identifier policy, which a processor configured otherwise does not make and no rerun changes, so the document is rejected *here* and the sender's fix is to name a schema this deployment will supply. A closed library's miss is a refusal, never "not found": the library is the deployment's whole configuration, so a schema outside it is one this deployment will not supply. A schema this processor **could not** obtain — a source that looks beyond its configuration (an origin, a directory) and found nothing at the reference, a host that did not answer, a fetch that timed out — leaves the document **undetermined**: nothing has read the schema, so nothing is known about whether the document conforms, and whoever holds the fix, for nothing at the reference the sender most likely does, a reference with nothing behind it being most often a typo. Both are distinguishable from the four categories, in the same report, located at the reference, and name their reason (not held, not permitted, too large; not found, unreachable, timed out), since a sender acting on a report needs to tell "your document is wrong" from "I will not look here" from "I could not look". A document with both an undetermined import and an ordinary error reports both, and a rejection settles the summary whatever else went unjudged ([TSON-DATA] §8.1). The library is populated through three mechanisms, in order of precedence:

- **Pre-loaded schemas.** The implementation ships the kernel as a pre-loaded entry and SHOULD ship the bundled libraries. Pre-loaded schemas are authoritative — their in-memory representation takes precedence over any external source (§3.4).
- **Registered schemas.** Applications register schemas before parsing documents that reference them, from local files, embedded resources, or any application-specific source.
- **Fetched schemas (optional).** Implementations MAY support fetching by URL; fetching MUST be explicitly enabled by the application — never the default — and is subject to §11.2. Production systems SHOULD register all required schemas at startup rather than fetch at runtime.

**Identity agreement.** Registration whose target identity differs from the canonical identity of the content's declared `!!id` is an error — the library MUST reject it as identity confusion. The comparison is over canonical identities, so scheme variance agrees; a differing host or path does not. Content with no `!!id` MAY be registered under an application-supplied identity (a development-mode convenience, §2.2.1); content with an `!!id` is registered under that identity and no other.

**Schema sources are schema documents.** Whenever the library is populated from a document — registered or fetched, for every target of `!!import` — that document MUST classify as a schema document ([TSON-DATA] §2.2): its root tagged `!schema`. A resolved form (resolver output, §8) is a schema document by that test and is nonetheless not a schema *source*: its derived fields cannot be verified from the document alone, it is non-canonical and not hash-pinnable under a source's identity, and reading it as the resolver's output is the caller's explicit choice (§8.1), never the library's inference. An implementation MUST NOT silently resolve an `!!import` to a document the caller has identified as a resolved form, and a resolved form read as output does not take derived indexes on trust: `@meta.synthetic`, `subtypes` and `disjoint` are discarded and recomputed; `chain.supertypes`, `chain.parents` and `@meta.application` are read as stated and verified; author-written annotations are preserved as data; and a value entry (§4.1) reads like any other entry — its value the data its type describes, validated against it, with the rule that no type position names it re-run on the map.


### 10.2 Hash-Pinned References

The hash-parameter URI convention — the algorithm-named query parameter, lowercase full-length hex, the query-is-hash-parameters-only rule, canonical identity, and the mismatch rule — is defined in [TSON-DATA] §2.2.1. This section defines how the library applies it. **Bytes identify a document, for distribution and pinning; names and parsed structure identify an entry, for typing** (§8.2): a whitespace edit to a schema changes its digest and every pin that names it, and none of its entries.

Library keys are canonical identities: a plain reference, its `http`/`https` variant, and its hash-pinned form all name the same entry — the pinned form additionally demands verification. When a hashed reference resolves, the implementation MUST verify the library's content against the declared hash before use; a mismatch is a **resolver error** — a verdict, unlike a refused or undetermined schema (§10.1), because the schema *was* obtained, its bytes were read, and they are not the bytes the document's reference commits to, which is a finding about the reference the document carries — and the library MUST NOT silently substitute mismatched content. When registration supplies a document whose declared `!!id` itself carries a hash, the implementation SHOULD verify at registration time.

**Hashes attach to the canonical entry.** Verification is a property of the canonical identity, not of each reference. Within a single resolution's reference closure (§3.4.1), the digests declared across all references to one identity form a set, and the set determines the identity's treatment. An empty set resolves unverified. A single digest is verified once against the entry's content, before any reference to the identity resolves; every reference — pinned and plain alike — then resolves to the verified entry. A plain reference alongside a pinned one is therefore not a conflict but an inheritance: it rides the pin, and it inherits the pin's failure — a verification failure applies to the identity for the whole resolution, and a plain reference MUST NOT proceed with content a declared digest has rejected. Two distinct digests for one identity is a resolver error: at most one describes the real bytes, and the library MUST NOT choose between them. A verified entry, once cached under its canonical identity, is immutable.
